satisfies the following:

(a)

W
has constant (graded) rank and, since
W
is contained in
\ker\mathcal{H}
whose rank is non-increasing when moving
A^{\prime}
away from
A_{0}
(in an open neighborhood) and since
W=\ker\mathcal{H}
at
A^{\prime}=A_{0}
, the rank of
\ker\mathcal{H}
must stay constant. Hence,
\ker\mathcal{H}=W
for
A^{\prime}\in U
.

(b)

W
contains a single representative of each
d_{A_{0}}
-cohomology class. Indeed, the map
q\colon W\rightarrow H_{A_{0}}
sending
\alpha\mapsto[\alpha]
is surjective, since Remark
2.29
defines a right inverse for
q
– a map
\rho\colon H_{A_{0}}\rightarrow W
satisfying
q\circ\rho=\mathrm{id}_{H_{A_{0}}}
. On the other hand, by (a)
W
has constant rank when
A^{\prime}
is changing, equal to the rank of
H_{A_{0}}
at
A^{\prime}=A_{0}
. Hence, the fact that
q
is a surjection implies that it is in fact an isomorphism.

Then, (a) together with (b) proves (5).

For (4), note that
\mathcal{H}
is diagonalizable at
A^{\prime}=A_{0}
and hence is diagonalizable for
A^{\prime}
in a neighborhood of
A_{0}
(since diagonalizability is an open condition). Thus,
\Omega=\ker\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathrm{im}\mathcal{H}
for
A^{\prime}\in U
, – splitting into zero-modes of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathrm{im}\mathcal{H}=\colon V
– the span of eigenforms of
\mathcal{H}
with nonzero eigenvalues. Operators
d_{A_{0}}
and
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
act on
V
(since they commute with
\mathcal{H}
). Moreover, one has

(80)

\mathrm{im}\mathcal{H}=\mathrm{im}(d_{A_{0}})\oplus\mathrm{im}(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}})

Indeed, the intersection of the summands on the right is zero: if
d_{A_{0}}\alpha=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\alpha=0
, then
\alpha\in\ker d_{A_{0}}\cap\ker d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}=\ker\mathcal{H}
, but since
\alpha\in V
it must be zero. Also, if
\alpha\in V
then
\alpha=d_{A_{0}}\beta+d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\gamma
with
\beta=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{-1}\alpha
and
\gamma=d_{A_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{-1}\alpha
(here we are using that
\mathcal{H}
is invertible on
V
). This proves that (
80
) is a direct sum.

Property (3) is obvious by a continuity argument: in the deformation of
A^{\prime}
away from
A_{0}
(in a small enough neighborhood), zero modes of
\mathcal{H}
are deformed to zero modes while eigenvectors with eigenvalues
\lambda>0
are deformed to eigenvectors with
\mathrm{Re}(\lambda)>0
.
∎

Definition 2.30
.

(1)

If
A_{0}
and
U
are as in Proposition
2.28
then for any
A^{\prime}\in U
we say that
(A_{0},A^{\prime})
is a pair of
close
flat connections.

(2)

If
(A_{0},A^{\prime})
is a pair of close flat connections, we call the space (
79
) the
space of
(A_{0},A^{\prime})
-harmonic forms
and denote it
\mathrm{Harm}_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}
.
We also call the associated decomposition (
74
) the
desynchronized Hodge decomposition
:

(81)

\Omega(M,\mathfrak{g})=\mathrm{Harm}_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}\oplus\mathrm{im}\,d_{A_{0}}\oplus\mathrm{im}\,d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}.

Further in this section we will suppress the subscript in
A_{0}
and just denote it
A
.

2.5.3.
Connection on the bundle of
(A,A^{\prime})
-harmonic forms

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
be an open neighborhood of the diagonal in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
obtained as the union of open sets
U
from Proposition
2.28
.
Consider the vector bundle
\mathrm{Harm}
over
\mathcal{U}
whose fiber over
(A,A^{\prime})
is the space of
(A,A^{\prime})
-harmonic forms
\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}}
.

Consider the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
on the bundle
\mathrm{Harm}
defined by infinitesimal parallel transport as follows. If
\chi\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}}
is a harmonic form, then:

(i)

For any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{d_{A}\mathrm{-closed}}
, when moving from
(A,A^{\prime})
to
(A+t\alpha,A^{\prime})
on
\mathcal{U}
,
\chi
transforms to

(82)

\chi-td^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\chi\quad\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A+t\alpha,A^{\prime}}.

(ii)

For any
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{d_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{-closed}}
, when moving from
(A,A^{\prime})
to
(A,A^{\prime}+s\beta)
on
\mathcal{U}
,
\chi
transforms to

(83)

\chi-sd_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\beta}\chi\quad\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}+s\beta}.

The formulae above are written in the first order in deformation parameters
s,t
.
One can consider
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
as a connection in the trivial bundle over
\mathcal{U}
with fiber
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
preserving the subbundle
\mathrm{Harm}
.
The covariant derivative operator associated with the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
is

(84)

\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}=\delta-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\Big(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}+d_{A}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}\Big)

with
\delta
the de Rham operator on
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.31
.

One can think of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
as a “shift-and-project” connection: its infinitesimal parallel transport takes
an
(A,A^{\prime})
-harmonic form
\chi
over
(A,A^{\prime})
and moves it to the
(A+t\alpha,A^{\prime}+s\beta)
-harmonic form
P_{A+t\alpha,A^{\prime}+s\beta}(\chi)
over
(A+t\alpha,A^{\prime}+s\beta)
. We note that this construction is reminiscent of the construction of Hitchin’s (projectively flat) connection in the Verlinde bundle
35
35

35

The vector bundle with fiber being the space of states of Chern-Simons theory on
\Sigma
, a.k.a. the space of WZW conformal blocks on
\Sigma
, a.k.a. the Verlinde space. See
[
APW91
]
.
over the moduli space of complex structures on a surface
\Sigma
.

One has the following:

Proposition 2.32
.

(a)

The curvature of the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
(restricted to harmonic forms) is

(85)

F_{\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}}=-P\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}P-P\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}P\\
\in\Omega^{1,1}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}})),

where we suppress subscripts in
P_{A,A^{\prime}},G_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
is flat on
A^{\prime}
-fixed and on
A
-fixed slices of
\mathcal{U}
.

(b)

The restriction of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
to the diagonal in
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
is a Euclidean connection – it preserves the Hodge inner product on harmonic forms.

(c)

Given a path
A_{t}
of flat connections
0\leq t\leq 1
, from
A
to
A^{\prime}
, the parallel transport of an
(A,A^{\prime})
-harmonic form
\chi
along the path
(A_{t},A^{\prime})
is
q(\chi)\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A^{\prime},A^{\prime}}
with
q\colon\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Harm}_{A^{\prime},A^{\prime}}
the orthogonal projection onto
A^{\prime}
-harmonic forms. Likewise, the parallel transport of
\chi
along the path
(A,A_{1-t})
is
p(\chi)\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A}
with
p\colon\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A}
the orthogonal projection onto
A
-harmonic forms.

Figure 4
.
Vertical and horizontal parallel transport to the diagonal on
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
(Proposition
2.32
(
c
)).

Proof.

(
a
):
Note that the connection (
84
) can be equivalently written as

\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}=\delta-G([d^{*},\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}]+[d,\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}])=\delta+G\delta\Delta.

Therefore, the curvature (on harmonic forms) is

(86)

(\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}})^{2}P=(\delta G\delta\Delta+G\,\delta\Delta\,G\,\delta\Delta)P\\
=\Big(G\Big(\underbrace{[d^{*},\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}]+[d,\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}]}_{-\delta\Delta}-K\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}P+P\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}K\\
-dG\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}P+P\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}Gd\Big)G\delta\Delta+G\delta\Delta G\delta\Delta\Big)P\\
=-P\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}\underbrace{KGd}_{GP_{\mathrm{coex}}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}P-P\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G\underbrace{dGd^{*}}_{P_{\mathrm{ex}}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}P,

which simplifies to (
85
).

(
b
): Infinitesimal parallel transport along the diagonal in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, from
(A,A)
to
(A+t\alpha,A+t\alpha)
transform a harmonic form
\chi\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A}
to
\chi^{\prime}=\chi-td^{*}_{A}G_{A,A}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\chi-td_{A}G_{A,A}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\chi
. Note that the three summands in
\chi^{\prime}
are mutually orthogonal and two of them are of order
t
, hence
||\chi^{\prime}||=||\chi||+O(t^{2})
. Therefore, if
A_{t}
is a path of flat connections and
\chi_{t}\in\mathrm{Harm}_{A_{t},A_{t}}
is the parallel transport of
\chi
along the corresponding path in the diagonal in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, then
\frac{d}{dt}||\chi_{t}||=0
.

(
c
): The form of the connection (
82
) implies that the parallel transport from
(A^{\prime},A^{\prime})
to
(A,A^{\prime})
transforms an
A^{\prime}
-harmonic form
\psi
to
\psi+d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}(\cdots)=\chi
. Hence, the reverse parallel transport transforms an
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
-closed form
\chi
to its projection
q(\chi)
onto
A^{\prime}
-harmonic forms. The case of moving from
(A,A^{\prime})
to
(A,A)
is analogous.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

The connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
is a rephrasing of the result of Proposition
2.15
and Remark
2.29
(and for paths considered in that Proposition and Remark,
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
gives the same parallel transport).

2.5.4.
Cohomology comparison map

Let
\mathbb{H}
be the “cohomology bundle” over
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
– the graded vector bundle with the fiber over
A
being
H^{\bullet}_{A}
. For a fixed
A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, let

\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}\colon=\mathcal{U}\cap(\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\{A^{\prime}\})
– the
A^{\prime}
-fixed slice of
\mathcal{U}
.
The connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
of Section
2.5.3
restricted to
\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}
induces (via the isomorphism
\mathrm{Harm}_{A,A^{\prime}}\cong H_{A}
,
\chi\mapsto[\chi]
) a flat connection
\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}
in
\mathbb{H}|_{\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}}
.

For
A,\widetilde{A}
a pair of close flat connections, close to
A^{\prime}
, we will call the parallel transport of
\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}
– the linear map

(87)

\mathfrak{B}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A;A^{\prime}}\colon H^{\bullet}_{A}\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{\widetilde{A}}

– the “cohomology comparison map.” Notice that due to the curvature of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
, this map depends nontrivially on
A^{\prime}
.

Sometimes we will need the cohomology comparison map restricted to cohomology in degree
1
; we will denote it
\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A;A^{\prime}}
.

Remark 2.34
.

In the special case
\widetilde{A}=A^{\prime}
the cohomology comparison map is

\mathfrak{B}_{A^{\prime}\leftarrow A;A^{\prime}}=[q]\colon H_{A}\rightarrow H_{A^{\prime}}

– the map induced in cohomology by the map
q
of Proposition
2.32
(
c
).

2.5.5.
Local exponential map for fixed
A^{\prime}
.

For a given smooth flat connection
A^{\prime}
, for
A\in\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}
, we have the desynchronized Hodge SDR data
(i_{A,A^{\prime}},p_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}})
. These induce, locally around
A^{\prime}
, a sum-over-trees exponential map that we denote
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\bullet,A^{\prime}}\colon T\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\supset V_{A}\to\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
. Contrary to the “global” exponential map
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\bullet,\bullet}
, the local exponential map
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\bullet,A^{\prime}}
is not equivariant with respect to the gauge group action on its argument. However, it satisfies the following “convolution” property:

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
\beta,\gamma\in T_{A}\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
such that
\beta,\gamma
and
\beta+\gamma
are in the domain of
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}
. Let
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\beta)
. Then

(88)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\beta+\gamma)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}((d\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}})_{\beta}(\gamma)).

Proof.

The proof follows from the combinatorics of tress and the homological perturbation lemma. Namely, one can expand the left hand side as a sum-over-trees map where edges are decorated by
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
and leaves are decorated either by
\beta
or by
\gamma
. On the other hand, one can expand the right hand side as a sum-over-trees map where edges are decorated by
K_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}
and leaves are decorated by
\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A;A^{\prime}}(\gamma)
. By the homological perturbation lemma, we have
K_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}=K_{A,A^{\prime}}-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\delta_{A,A^{\prime}}K_{A,A^{\prime}}+\ldots
, which we can represent as a sum over ways to plug in a forest of trees into an edge, with leaves decorated by
\beta
and edges decorated by
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Finally,
(d\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}})_{\beta}(\gamma)
is itself given as a sum over trees with edges labeled by
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
, one leaf labeled
\gamma
and all other leaves labeled
\beta
(see Remark
2.16
). In this way, one also on the right hand side obtains a sum over trees with edges labeled by
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
and leaves either labeled
\beta
or
\gamma
. The numerical prefactor of each such tree is the same on both sides and given by
(-1)^{n-1}/|\operatorname{Aut}T|
where
n
is the number of leaves and
\operatorname{Aut}T
are the automorphisms of
T
respecting the decorations of leaves.
∎

This then leads to the following explicit description of the Grothendieck connection on
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
:
Let
\alpha,\beta\in T_{A}\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, and
\widetilde{A}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\beta)
. Denote
\widetilde{\alpha}
the parallel transport of
\alpha
from
A\to\widetilde{A}
.

Proposition 2.36
.

We have

(89)

\widetilde{\alpha}=(d\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}})_{\beta}(\alpha-\beta).

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\widetilde{\alpha}=\widetilde{\varphi}^{-1}_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{A,A^{\prime}}
and Proposition
2.35
by choosing
\gamma
such that
\alpha=\gamma+\beta
.
∎

By restricting to harmonic forms and passing to cohomology, we obtain a local exponential map
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon V_{A^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}
, defined on an open subset
V_{A^{\prime}}
of the cohomology bundle
\mathbb{H}\big|_{\mathcal{{U}_{A}}}\to\mathcal{U}_{A^{\prime}}
. Associated to this map is a partial fiber bundle connection, whose parallel transport can be defined as follows: Let
\alpha,\beta\in H^{1}_{A}
and
\widetilde{A}=\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)
. Then the parallel transport of
\alpha
from
A
to
\widetilde{A}
is given by the cohomology comparison map

(90)

\widetilde{\alpha}=\mathfrak{B}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha-\beta)

since by Remark
2.33
the parallel transport of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
from
A
to
\widetilde{A}=\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\beta)
is given by
(d\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}})_{\beta}
.
Cf. also Definition
4.7
and Remark
4.8
.

3.
Perturbative Chern-Simons partition function in the BV formalism

In this section we recall the definition of the perturbative Chern-Simons partition function at an arbitrary reference flat connection
A_{0}
given in
[
CM08
]
(a detailed review can be found also in
[
Mne19
]
,
[
Wer22
]
), and extend this to the definition of the desynchronized partition function which uses as gauge fixing operator the codifferential
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
instead of
d^{*}_{A_{0}}
. Let
G
be a simple, compact and simply connected Lie group and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
an
\mathrm{ad}
-invariant pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
P
be a principal
G
-bundle on a 3-manifold
M
, we will assume that a trivialization
36
36

36

Our assumptions are such that trivializations are guaranteed to exist. See for instance
[
Fre95
]
.
of
P
has been fixed:
P\cong M\times G
. We can therefore identify connections with
\mathfrak{g}
-valued 1-forms. Our convention for the Chern-Simons action
S_{CS}\colon\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{R}
is

(91)

S_{CS}(A)=\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\langle A,dA\rangle+\frac{1}{6}\langle A,[A,A]\rangle.

Its critical points are the flat connections, i.e. those 1-forms
A_{0}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
satisfying

(92)

dA_{0}+\frac{1}{2}[A_{0},A_{0}]=0.

For a flat connection
A_{0}\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, we denote the twisted de Rham differential by

(93)

d_{A_{0}}=d+[A_{0},\cdot]\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(M,\mathfrak{g})

and the
A_{0}
-twisted de Rham cohomology by
H^{\bullet}_{d_{A_{0}}}(M,\operatorname{Ad}P)
.

3.1.
Perturbative partition function

We can now proceed with the definition of the perturbative Chern-Simons partition function at
A_{0}
. Formally, we want to define it as the perturbative evaluation of the BV pushforward

Z_{\mathsf{h}}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})=\int_{\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\in\operatorname{im}\mathsf{h}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A_{0}+\mathsf{a}+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We first define the partition function with gauge fixing operator
\mathsf{h}=d^{*}_{A_{0}}
, and then comment on changing the gauge fixing operator.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
A_{0}
be a flat connection on
M
, and
g
a Riemannian metric on
M
. The Chern-Simons partition function at
A_{0}
with gauge fixing operator
d^{*}_{A_{0}}
is defined by

(94)

Z(A_{0},\mathsf{a};g)\colon=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A_{0})}\tau(A_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi(A_{0};g)}\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\left(\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{-\chi(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A_{0};g}(\mathsf{a})\right)\\
\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\big(S_{CS}(A_{0})+\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\langle\mathsf{a},l_{n}(\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{a})\rangle\big)}\cdot\mathrm{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}[1])^{*}[[\hbar]]

– a formal half-density on de Rham cohomology twisted by
A_{0}
.
37
37

37

Note that there is no sign ambiguity in the square root line bundle
\mathrm{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})
, since by Poincaré duality it can be expressed as
\mathrm{Det}(H^{0})\otimes(\mathrm{Det}(H^{1}))^{*}
.

Here:

•

S_{CS}(A_{0})
is the value of Chern-Simons action (
91
) on
A_{0}
.

•

\tau(A_{0})\in\mathrm{Det}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})
is the Ray-Singer torsion of
M
with local system
A_{0}
.
\tau(A_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathrm{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})
is its square root.

•

\psi(A_{0};g)
is the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer eta-invariant of the operator
{L_{-}\colon=*d_{A_{0}}+d_{A_{0}}*}
acting on forms of odd degree.

•

The sum ranges over connected 3-valent graphs (“Feynman graphs”)
\Gamma
with leaves (loose half-edges) allowed.
\chi(\Gamma)
is the Euler characteristic of the graph and
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
is the automorphism group. The weight of a graph
\Gamma
is a polynomial in
\mathsf{a}
defined as

(95)

\Phi_{\Gamma,A_{0};g}(\mathsf{a})=\\
\int_{\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{V}(M)}\left\langle\prod_{\mathrm{leaves}\;l}\pi^{*}_{v(l)}i_{A_{0}}(\mathsf{a})\prod_{\mathrm{edges}\,e=(uv)}\pi_{uv}^{*}\eta_{A_{0}}\prod_{\mathrm{short\;loops}\;e=(vv)}\pi_{v}^{*}\eta_{A_{0}}^{\Delta},\bigotimes_{\mathrm{vertices}}f\right\rangle,

where:

–

\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{V}(M)
is the Fulton-MacPherson-Axelrod-Singer compactification of the configuration space of
V=\#\{\mathrm{vertices}\}
points on
M
.

–

\pi_{uv}\colon\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{V}(M)\rightarrow\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{2}(M)
is the map forgetting the positions of all points except points
u
and
v
; similarly,

\pi_{v}\colon\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{V}(M)\rightarrow M
is the map forgetting all points except
v
.

–

The propagator
\eta_{A_{0}}\in\Omega^{2}(\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{2}(M),\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
is minus the integral kernel of the operator

(96)

K_{A_{0}}=d^{*}_{A_{0}}(\Delta_{A_{0}}+P_{\mathrm{Harm}})^{-1}

– the Hodge chain homotopy between
d_{A_{0}}
and projection to harmonic forms.

–

\eta_{A_{0}}^{\Delta}\in\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g})
is the appropriately renormalized evaluation of
\eta_{A_{0}}
on the diagonal.
38
38

38

It is the term
L^{cont}
in
[
AS91
]
, formula (PL5). It is the limit
\lim_{y\rightarrow x}(\eta_{A_{0}}(x,y)-(\cdots))
with
(\cdots)
the singular part of
\eta
at the diagonal.

–

i_{A_{0}}
maps a cohomology class to its harmonic representative.

–

f\in\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}
is the structure tensor of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
.

–

\langle,\rangle
is the inner product on
\mathfrak{g}
extended to
\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes\#\{\mathrm{half-edges}\}}
.

–

In (
95
), the first product is over leaves of
\Gamma
, with
v(l)
the vertex incident to the leaf; the second product is over edges connecting
distinct
vertices
u,v
; the third product is over “short loops” – edges connecting a vertex
v
to itself.

Remark 3.2
.

One can split the partition function according to “loop number” as
Z(A_{0},\mathsf{a})=Z^{(0)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})Z^{(1)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})Z^{(\geq 2)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})
,
where

\displaystyle Z^{(0)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(S_{CS}(A_{0})+\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr_{conn}},\,l(\Gamma)=0}\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A_{0};m}(\mathsf{a})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(S_{CS}(A_{0})+\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{(n+1)!}\langle\mathsf{a},l_{n}(\mathsf{a},\ldots,\mathsf{a})\rangle\right)\right),

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle:=\tau(A_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi(A_{0};g)}\cdot\exp\left(\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr_{conn}},\,l(\Gamma)=1}\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A_{0};g}(\mathsf{a})\right)

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}[1])^{*},

\displaystyle Z^{(\geq 2)}(A_{0},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{Gr_{conn}},\,l(\Gamma)\geq 2}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)}}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A_{0};g}(\mathsf{a})\right)\in\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}[1])^{*}[[\hbar]].

Here
l(\Gamma)
is the number of loops in a connected graph.

The reason for excluding tree (0-loop) diagrams from the sum in (
94
) is that they come with a factor of
1/\hbar
so after taking exponential we would obtain unbounded negative powers oh
\hbar
. Instead, they are included in the prefactor in the form of the induced
L_{\infty}
operations
l_{n}
.

Theorem 3.3
.

The perturbative partition function is closed with respect to the BV Laplacian on zero modes,

(97)

\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}Z_{A_{0}}(\mathsf{a})=0.

We refer to
[
Wer22
, Section 3.4.2]
for the proof. It is in turn an adaptation of the proof of Lemma 4.11 from
[
CMR17
]
, using Stokes’ theorem for configuration space integrals representing Feynman weights.
Also, the case
A_{0}=0
is a part of Theorem 1 in
[
CM08
]
.

Remark 3.4
.

(i)

If the flat connection
A_{0}
is
irreducible
, then
H^{0}_{A_{0}}=H^{3}_{A_{0}}=0
.
An elementary degree count then shows that
Z(A_{0},\mathsf{a})
depends only on the 1-form component of
\mathsf{a}
. In particular, in this case (
97
) holds trivially.

(ii)

If
[A_{0}]
is a smooth point in the moduli space of flat connections, then operations
l_{n}
on
H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}
vanish (i.e., the tree graphs in (
94
) cancel out).

3.2.
Desynchronized partition function

Let
\mathsf{h}
be a good gauge fixing operator for
A_{0}
, and
r_{\mathsf{h}}=(i_{\mathsf{h}},p_{\mathsf{h}},K_{\mathsf{h}})
the corresponding SDR data. The goal of this subsection is to define the “desynchronized” perturbative partition function, heuristically given by the BV pushforward

(98)

Z_{A_{0},\mathsf{h}}(\mathsf{a})=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\int_{\alpha\in\operatorname{im}\mathsf{h}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A_{0}+i_{\mathsf{h}}(\mathsf{a})+\alpha)}\sqrt{\mu^{\prime\prime}}\bigg|_{\mathcal{L}}

where
\sqrt{\mu}=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\sqrt{\mu^{\prime\prime}}
is the formal Lebesgue half-density on
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[1]
and
\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}},\sqrt{\mu^{\prime\prime}}
the Lebesgue
half-densities on
H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}[1]\cong\ker\mathcal{H}
and
\operatorname{im}d_{A_{0}}\oplus\operatorname{im}\mathsf{h}
respectively. For the remainder of this section we fix
\mathsf{h}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
, for some flat connection
A^{\prime}
close to
A_{0}
in the sense of Definition
2.30
.

We then define the desynchronized partition function analogously to the synchronized case:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
(A,A^{\prime})
be a pair of close, smooth flat connections. Then we define the
desynchronized partition function

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}\cdot\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])^{*}[[\hbar]]

as the product

(99)

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=Z^{(0)}_{A,A^{\prime}}\,Z^{(1)}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\,Z^{(\geq 2)}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})

where
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}^{(0)}:=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}
and

(100)

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\mathsf{a}):=e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}\exp\left(\sum_{\Gamma\in Gr_{conn},l(\Gamma)=1}\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\right)\\
\in\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])^{*},

(101)

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}^{(\geq 2)}(\mathsf{a}):=\exp\left(\sum_{\Gamma\in Gr_{conn},l(\Gamma)\geq 2}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\right)\\
\in\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])^{*}[[\hbar]].

The Feynman weights
\Phi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
are defined as in (
95
), where we replace the integral kernel (
96
) of
K_{A}
by the integral kernel of

(102)

K_{A,A^{\prime}}=d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}\circ(\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}})^{-1}

and the map
i_{A}
with
i_{A,A^{\prime}}
.

Notice that since
A
is smooth, there are no trees in the zero-loop part – their weights vanish by the smoothness assumption.

3.2.1.
Digression: Path integral computation of desynchronized 1-loop part

The abelian part of the path integral (
98
) is

(103)

I_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\int_{\alpha\in\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime*}}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha,d_{A}\alpha\rangle}\sqrt{\mu^{\prime\prime}}\bigg|_{\mathcal{L}}.

Perturbative formula (
100
) corresponds to evaluating the path integral (
103
) to

(104)

I_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon=\tau_{A}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\psi_{A}}.

In this digression we want to explain why this is a good definition of the r.h.s. of (
103
).
Namely, naive evaluation of this path integral would go along the following lines.
For a subspace
V\subset\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
and an isomorphism
F\colon V\to V
we set

\int_{\alpha\in V}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}(\alpha,F\alpha)_{H}}\mu_{H}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\mathrm{sign}F}\operatorname{Sdet}_{V}^{\frac{1}{2}}F

where
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}
denotes the Hodge inner product

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})_{H}=\int_{M}\langle\alpha_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}*\alpha_{2}\rangle

and the signature and superdeterminant have to be understood in a regularized sense. Looking at (
103
), the map
*d_{A}
maps
d_{A^{\prime}}
-coexact forms to
d_{A}
-coexact forms, so it is not an endomorphism of
\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}
. The orthogonal (with respect to the Hodge inner product) projector to
\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}
is
K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}
, so we obtain

(105)

I_{A,A^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\,e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sign}K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A}}\operatorname{Sdet}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A}).

We claim that this coincides with the following definition:

Lemma 3.6
.

For a pair of close flat connections
(A,A^{\prime})
,
I_{A,A^{\prime}}
can be expressed as

(106)

I_{A,A^{\prime}}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\psi_{A^{\prime}}}\det(\mathfrak{B}_{A\leftarrow A^{\prime};A^{\prime}})^{1/2}\tau_{A^{\prime}}^{1/2}\operatorname{Sdet}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}\left(1+K_{A^{\prime}}\operatorname{ad}_{\beta}\right)\in\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}H^{\bullet}_{A},

where

•

\psi_{A^{\prime}}
is the eta-invariant of
*d_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A^{\prime}}*
,

•

\mathfrak{B}_{A\leftarrow A^{\prime};A^{\prime}}\colon H^{\bullet}_{A^{\prime}}\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{A}
is the cohomology comparison map of Section
2.5.4
.

•

\operatorname{Sdet}
denotes a zeta-regularized superdeterminant,

•

\beta=A-A^{\prime}
is the difference between the two flat connections.

Proof.

We have on
\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}
that
K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}=\mathrm{id}
and therefore

\mathrm{id}+K_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad_{\beta}}=\mathrm{id}+K_{A^{\prime}}(d_{A}-d_{A^{\prime}})=K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A}.

Also,

\det(\mathfrak{B}_{A\leftarrow A^{\prime};A^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau_{A^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\operatorname{Sdet}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}*d_{A^{\prime}}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\det(\mathfrak{B}_{A\leftarrow A^{\prime};A^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau_{A^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname{Sdet}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}\left(1+K_{A^{\prime}}\operatorname{ad}_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\operatorname{Sdet}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(*d_{A^{\prime}}K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\operatorname{Sdet}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(*d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A^{\prime}}*G_{A^{\prime}}d_{A})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\operatorname{Sdet}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(*d_{A^{\prime}}*G_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\operatorname{Sdet}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A})

where we have used that the Green’s function
G_{A^{\prime}}
commutes with both the the Hodge star and
d_{A^{\prime}}
.
39
39

39

In principle there could be a multiplicative anomaly when combining the regularized superdeterminants, but here it is absent because the equality is trivially true for
\beta=0
and we are restricting to small
\beta
.

For the phase, we note that the spectrum of
K_{A^{\prime}}d_{A^{\prime}}*d_{A}
is obtained from the spectrum of
*d_{A^{\prime}}
through continuous deformation where none of the
real parts of the eigenvalues crosses zero, therefore any regularization of the signature will yield the same result.
∎

However, it turns out that we have the following:

Lemma 3.7
.

The expression (
106
) for
I_{A,A^{\prime}}
is independent of
A^{\prime}
and depends on
g
only through
\psi_{A^{\prime}}
. In particular,
I_{A,A^{\prime}}=I_{A,A}=\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}_{A}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\psi_{A}}
.

The proof is a long computation. Crucially, the non-flatness of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
means that the cohomology comparison map
\mathfrak{B}_{A\leftarrow A^{\prime};A^{\prime}}
depends both on
A^{\prime}
and
g
, this dependence precisely cancels the dependence of
\operatorname{Sdet}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\operatorname{im}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}}\left(1+K_{A^{\prime}}\operatorname{ad}_{\beta}\right)
on
A^{\prime}
and
g
.

4.
Properties of the desynschronized partition function

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
14
which we split up in several subsections. Throughout this section
A
and
A
is a pair of close smooth irreducible flat connections.

4.1.
Gauge invariance

We first discuss the impact of gauge transformations on
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
. Note that the gauge transformation
A\mapsto{}^{\mathrm{g}}A
induces an isomorphism
H_{A}^{\bullet}\cong H_{{}^{\mathrm{g}}A}^{\bullet}
by the adjoint action on cohomology classes.

Proposition 4.1
.

We have that
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
is invariant under “diagonal” gauge transformations
(A,A^{\prime},\mathsf{a})\mapsto({}^{\mathrm{g}}A,{}^{\mathrm{g}}A^{\prime},{}^{\mathrm{g}}\mathsf{a})
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that all the ingredients of
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}
are gauge equivariant. I.e., we have
K_{{}^{\mathrm{g}}A}({}^{g}\omega)={}^{\mathrm{g}}(K_{A}\omega)
and
\iota_{{}^{\mathrm{g}}A}[{}^{g}\omega]={}^{\mathrm{g}}(\iota_{A}\omega).
Finally, we contract tensors using the
G
-invariant pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
. ∎

4.2.
Horizontality w.r.t. Grothendieck connection (changing the kinetic operator)

In the next theorem we prove that a shift in the kinetic operator can be expressed as a shift of the zero mode (or vice versa).

Let
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)=A+\delta_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)\colon\;U\rightarrow\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
be the sum-over-trees exponential map (
46
), determined by the
SDR data
associated to the SDR data
r_{\mathsf{h}}=(i_{\mathsf{h}},p_{\mathsf{h}},K_{\mathsf{h}})
corresponding to the gauge-fixing operator
\mathsf{h}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
. The map
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)
, as a function of
\alpha
, is defined on some open neighborhood
U
of zero in
H^{1}_{A}
.

In this section we will denote for brevity

(107)

\widetilde{A}\colon=\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha).

Denote

(108)

B\colon=\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A;A^{\prime}}\colon H^{1}_{A}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\widetilde{A}}

the cohomology comparison map in degree
1
.

Remark 4.2
.

The map (
108
) coincides with the differential of
\pi\circ\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)
in the last argument, with
\pi\colon\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
the quotient by gauge transformations. This follows from the fact that

(109)

\displaystyle i_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}\circ B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{A,A^{\prime}}-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)}i_{A,A^{\prime}}+\cdots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)\;\colon H_{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Harm}^{1}_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}},

cf. Remarks
2.17
,
2.33
.

Theorem 4.3
.

We have that
the desynchronized partition function satisfies

(110)

\det(B^{\vee})\circ Z_{\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha),A^{\prime}}(B(\mathsf{a}))=Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha+\mathsf{a})

where

\mathsf{a}
and
\alpha
denote variables in an open neighborhood of zero in
H^{1}_{A}
.

The proof of this theorem relies on the following fact about the dependence of the Ray-Singer torsion on the local system that we were unable to locate in the literature:

Proposition 4.4
.

Ray-Singer torsion satisfies

(111)

\det(B^{\vee})\circ\tau_{\widetilde{A}}^{1/2}=\tau_{A}^{1/2}\exp\sum_{\gamma}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma)|}\Phi_{\gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha).

Here
\gamma
runs over 1-loop connected trivalent graphs.

We give the proof in Appendix
D.2
.

Sketch of proof of Theorem
4.3
.

The r.h.s. of (
110
) is
e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{\frac{1}{2}}
times
the exponential of the sum of connected Feynman graphs
\Gamma
with
l\geq 1
loops, with leaves decorated by either
i_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
or
i_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)
and edges decorated by
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
. Given such a graph
\Gamma
, one can represent it – in a unique way – as a smaller graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
with leaves decorated by subtrees
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
of the original graph
\Gamma
, where

•

Subtrees
X_{i}
have a single leaf decorated by
\mathsf{a}
; all the rest are decorated by
\alpha
.

•

Subtrees
Y_{j}
have all leaves decorated by
\alpha
.

•

If a vertex of
\Gamma^{\prime}
has more than one incident leaves, they must all be decorated by
X
-subtrees.

One can think of
\Gamma^{\prime}
as
\Gamma
with subtrees
\{X_{i}\}
,
\{Y_{j}\}
collapsed, each tree to its root. We will also denote
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
the graph obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by removing all
Y
-leaves and merging the internal edges incident to them.

Explicit construction of
\Gamma^{\prime}
:
\Gamma
can be thought of a trivalent graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with no leaves, with several rooted trees
T_{1},\ldots,T_{N}
plugged into the edges of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
. Starting from each leaf of
\Gamma
decorated by
\mathsf{a}
(which belongs to some tree
T_{k}
), draw the shortest path along edges connecting it to the root of
T_{k}
, call it an “
\mathsf{a}
-path.” The graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained by taking all the edges of
\Gamma
which are either (a) non-separating (cutting the edge does not make the graph disconnected) or (b) have at least
2

\mathsf{a}
-paths passing through them; together with each vertex involved we take its neighborhood in
\Gamma
, producing leaves. The graph
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma^{\prime}
is disjoint and consists of
X
-subtrees (those containing an
\mathsf{a}
-path) and
Y
-subtrees (those not containing an
\mathsf{a}
-path).

Figure 5
.
A Feynman graph
\Gamma
with
X
- and
Y
-subtrees and the resulting
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
graphs.
\mathsf{a}
-paths are shown in red; edges belonging to several
\mathsf{a}
paths are thick red edges.

The sum over
\Gamma
can be represented as a sum over graphs
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Summation over possible subtrees
X

on a leaf of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
yields

(112)

i_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}(B(\mathsf{a}))=i_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)}i_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})+\cdots,

cf. (
109
).
Summation over inserting
k\geq 0

Y
-subtrees into an edge
e
of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
results in decorating that edge with the chain homotopy

(113)

K_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}=K_{A,A^{\prime}}-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta_{A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)}K_{A,A^{\prime}}+\cdots

Formulae (
112
), (
113
) are the homological perturbation theory expressions for the deformation of an SDR data
(i,p,K)
for the deformation retraction
\Omega(M,\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow H_{A}
induced by a deformation of the differential from
d_{A}
to
d_{\widetilde{A}}
,
see Appendix
A
for details.

Thus, the sum over Feynman graphs
\Gamma
in the r.h.s. of (
110
) equals the sum over Feynman graphs
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
in the l.h.s. of (
110
). There is one correction: one-loop graphs
\Gamma
with leaves decorated only by
\alpha
(no
\mathsf{a}
) were omitted in this correspondence, since they result in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
being a loop with no vertices, which is not a legitimate graph. Thus we have

(114)

\exp\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma^{\prime\prime})-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}(B(\mathsf{a}))\cdot\exp\sum_{\gamma\;\mathrm{1-loop}}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\gamma)|}\Phi_{\gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha)\\
=\exp\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\alpha+\mathsf{a}).

Together with (
111
) this implies (
110
).
∎

Corollary 4.5

(Infinitesimal variation of kinetic operator)
.

Let
A_{t}
be a curve of flat connections such that
\dot{A}_{0}=i_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\alpha)
and let
B_{t}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{A_{t}\leftarrow A_{0};A^{\prime}}\colon H^{1}_{A_{0}}\rightarrow H^{1}_{A_{t}}
be the cohomology comparison map in degree
1
. Then

(115)

\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\Big(\det(B_{t}^{\vee})\circ Z_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}(B_{t}(\mathsf{a}))\Big)=\left\langle\alpha,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\right\rangle Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a}).

Proof 1.

Follows immediately from Theorem
4.3
by setting
A_{t}=\varphi_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(t\alpha)
and taking the derivative of both sides in
t
at
t=0
.
∎

One can also prove (
115
) by a standalone combinatorial argument.

Proof 2.

One has the following formulae for the infinitesimal variation of
i,K
:

(116)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}K_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle-K_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{i_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\alpha)}K_{A_{0},A^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}(B_{t}(\mathsf{a}))=

\displaystyle-K_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{i_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\alpha)}i_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a}).

These imply that the l.h.s. of (
115
) is the sum over graphs
\Gamma
, where either (i) one edge is split into two by an insertion of leaf decorated by
\alpha
, or (ii) one
\mathsf{a}
-leaf is replaced by a subtree consisting of an
\mathsf{a}
-leaf meeting an
\alpha
-leaf and continuing with an edge (Figure
6
).

Figure 6
.
Variation of a Feynman graph
\Gamma
under a harmonic shift of
A
: local picture on the graph.

Additionally, one has a special graph – the one-loop graph with a single
\alpha
-leaf (the “tadpole”), arising from the variation of
\tau_{A_{t}}^{1/2}
(Proposition
4.4
)
It is easy to see that the r.h.s. of (
115
) yields exactly the same graphs.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The r.h.s. of (
115
) can also be written as a BV-exact term

(117)

\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}\rangle Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\Big).

The expression in brackets is a formal half-density on
H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}
of ghost degree
-1
. Expanding
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{a}^{1}+\mathsf{a}^{2}
, with
\mathsf{a}^{k}\in H^{k}_{A_{0}}[1-k]
, we can write the expression in brackets as

\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}^{2}\rangle Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a}^{1})
.

Indeed, one has

(118)

\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}\rangle Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\Big)=\\
=-\underbrace{\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}\rangle}_{0}\cdot Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})-\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}\rangle\cdot\underbrace{\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})}_{0}-\Big\{\left\langle\alpha,\mathsf{a}\right\rangle,Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\Big\}\\
=\left\langle\alpha,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\right\rangle Z_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})

– the r.h.s. of (
115
), as claimed.

Consider the graded vector bundle
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
with fiber over
A
being the space of formal half-densities on cohomology

(119)

\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])\cong\mathrm{Det}(H^{1}_{A})^{*}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{Sym}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])^{*}.

The flat connection
\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}
in the cohomology bundle (Section
2.5.4
) induces a flat connection in
\mathcal{D}
which by abuse of notations we will also denote
\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}
.

Let
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
be the projection onto the first factor in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.7

(Partial Grothendieck connection)
.

One has a partial connection
\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}
on the bundle
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
defined by

(120)

(\widetilde{\nabla}^{G})_{(\chi,0)}\xi(\mathsf{a})=\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}_{\chi}\xi(\mathsf{a})-\left\langle[\chi],\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\right\rangle\xi(\mathsf{a})

for any
\chi\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}_{A,A^{\prime}}
and
\xi(\mathsf{a})
a section of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}
.
Here
(\chi,0)
is a tangent vector to
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
at a point
(A,A^{\prime})
.

Thus (
120
) allows to differentiate sections of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}
in the direction of infinitesimal harmonic shifts of
A
.

Remark 4.8
.

The partial connection (
120
) is induced (via pushforward of half-densities) from the fiber bundle partial connection on the cohomology bundle
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathbb{H}
over
\mathcal{U}

defined by the parallel transport

(121)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}H_{A}^{\bullet}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathbb{H}|_{A,A^{\prime}}&\rightarrow&H^{\bullet}_{\widetilde{A}}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathbb{H}|_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}\\
\mathsf{a}&\mapsto&\widetilde{\mathsf{a}}=\mathfrak{B}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a}-\alpha)\end{array}

with
\alpha\in H^{1}_{A}
sufficiently small and
\widetilde{A}
as in (
107
). We remark that the parallel transport (
121
) satisfies

(122)

\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\varphi_{\widetilde{A},A^{\prime}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{a}}).

for
\mathsf{a}\in H^{1}_{A}
sufficiently small.

Corollary 4.9

(Horizontality with respect to the partial Grothendieck connection)
.

One has

(123)

\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}=0.

This is just a rephrasing of Corollary
4.5
.

4.3.
Changing the gauge-fixing operator

Theorem 4.10

(Changing the gauge-fixing operator)
.

We have that, for
A_{0}^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}_{1}
flat connections close to a flat connection
A

(124)

Z_{A,A_{1}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=Z_{A,A^{\prime}_{0}}(\mathsf{a})+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}R_{A,A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathsf{a}).

with
R_{A,A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathsf{a})
a formal half-density on
H^{\bullet}_{A}[1]
given by (
128
) below.

This is an immediate consequence of Proposition
4.11
below, by integrating over a path
A^{\prime}_{t}
from
A^{\prime}_{0}
to
A^{\prime}_{1}
.

Let us consider the effect of an infinitesimal change of
A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime}+\delta A^{\prime}
on
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Consider the following endomorphisms of
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
:

(125)

\Lambda=K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}},\quad\mathbb{I}=G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}},\quad\mathbb{P}=\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}.

They arise in the first-order deformation of the SDR data
(i,p,K)_{A,A^{\prime}}
resulting from the deformation of
A^{\prime}
:

(126)

\displaystyle\delta_{A^{\prime}}K_{A,A^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle[d_{A},\Lambda]+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}+\mathbb{I}P_{A,A^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\delta_{A^{\prime}}i_{A,A^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle-d_{A}\mathbb{I}i_{A,A^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\delta_{A^{\prime}}p_{A,A^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle-p_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}d_{A},

cf. Lemma
54
.
Here
P_{A,A^{\prime}}=i_{A,A^{\prime}}p_{A,A^{\prime}}
is the projection onto
(A,A^{\prime})
-harmonic forms. We note that
\mathbb{I}
and
\mathbb{P}
are mutually transpose w.r.t. Poincaré pairing on forms and cohomology, while
\Lambda
is symmetric w.r.t. Poincaré pairing.

Proposition 4.11
.

For
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
a pair of close flat connections, the variation of

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
with respect to variation of
A^{\prime}
is given by

(127)

\delta_{A^{\prime}}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\left(r_{A,A^{\prime};\delta A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\right)

where
r_{A,A^{\prime};\delta A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
is the sum of connected Feynman graphs with one marked edge decorated by
\Lambda
or one marked leaf decorated by
\mathbb{I}
.

Note that if
A^{\prime}_{t}
is a path from
A^{\prime}_{0}
to
A^{\prime}_{1}
, integrating (
127
) we obtain (
124
) with

(128)

R_{A,A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1}}(\mathsf{a})=\int_{0}^{1}dt\,r_{A,A^{\prime}_{t};\dot{A}^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})Z_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a}).

Sketch of proof of Proposition
4.11
.

We have

(129)

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})^{-1}\,\delta_{A^{\prime}}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\sum_{\Gamma\;\mathrm{connected}}\delta_{A^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})

where we denoted

\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\frac{(-i\hbar)^{-\chi(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\Phi_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\int_{\overline{\mathrm{Conf}}_{V}(M)}\omega_{\Gamma}

where
\omega_{\Gamma}
is the integrand of (
95
) in
(A,A^{\prime})
-gauge, normalized with an appropriate power of
\hbar
and a combinatorial factor.

The rest of the proof follows the arguments of
[
CM08
]
,
[
CMR17
]
.

Variation of the value of a Feynman graph in
A^{\prime}
is the sum over edges and leaves of the graph of replacing that leaf with its variation, which contains terms
[d,\Lambda]
,
d\mathbb{I}i
, see (
126
). Then one uses Stokes’ theorem on the configuration space to move the differential
d
from the marked edge or leaf to other graph edges. The result is:
\delta_{A^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
is a sum of

(i)

Graphs
\Gamma^{(1)}
obtained by decorating one edge of
\Gamma
with
\Lambda
and one other edge by
[d,K]=1-P
.

(ii)

Graphs
\Gamma^{(2)}
obtained by decorating one leaf of
\Gamma
with
\mathbb{I}i(\mathsf{a})
and one edge by
1-P
.

(iii)

Graphs
\Gamma^{(3)}
obtained by decorating one edge of
\Gamma
with
\mathbb{I}P
.

Here we omit the subscripts in
d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}},P_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
The contribution of
1
on an edge can be seen as an integral over the principal boundary stratum of the configuration space (arising when we use the Stokes’ theorem as above) corresponding to a collapse of two points.

Figure 7
.
Examples of
\Gamma^{(1)}
,
\Gamma^{(2)}
,
\Gamma^{(3)}
. (We don’t write the contribution
1
on dashed edges as it cancels in the sum over graphs.)

The contributions of
1
on edges cancel out in the sum over graphs as a consequence of Jacobi identity (or, equivalently, as a consequence of the classical master equation on BV Chern-Simons action).

Hidden boundary strata of the configuration space do not contribute by the standard arguments, see
[
CM08
]
.

This implies that

(130)

\sum_{\Gamma}\delta_{A^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma}=i\hbar\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Big)+\Big\{\sum_{\Gamma^{\#}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\#}},\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Big\}\right).

Here
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\#}
runs over connected graphs.
\Gamma^{\prime}
runs over connected grants with either (a) one edge decorated by
\Lambda
or (b) one leaf decorated by
\mathbb{I}i(\mathsf{a})
.

Indeed, the
\delta_{A^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
is the sum of decorated graphs
\Gamma^{(i)}
as above,
i=1,2,3
(we can ignore the contributions of 1 on edges, as discussed above). Removing the
P
-edge either

(A)

disconnects
\Gamma^{(i)}
into a graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
containing
\Lambda
or
\mathbb{I}
and a graph
\Gamma^{\#}
, or

(B)

does not disconnect the graph, and gives a connected graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
containing
\Lambda
or
\mathbb{I}
.

Adding back the
P
-edge (in all possible ways) amounts to applying
-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}
to
\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
in the case (B) and amounts to evaluating the Poisson bracket
-i\hbar\{\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\#}},\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\}
in the case (A).

The factor
i\hbar=(-1)(-i\hbar)
in the r.h.s. of (
130
) stems from the fact that Feynman weights contain the factor
(-i\hbar)^{-\chi(\Gamma)}
and from the inclusion-exclusion formula for the Euler characteristic: adding back the
P
-edge changes
-\chi
by
+1
. The extra minus comes from the Stokes’ theorem on the configuration space.

From (
130
), denoting
\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
by
r(\mathsf{a})
, we obtain

Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})^{-1}\,\delta_{A^{\prime}}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=i\hbar\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}r(\mathsf{a})+\Big\{\sum_{\Gamma^{\#}}\overline{\Phi}_{\Gamma^{\#}},r(\mathsf{a})\Big\}\right).

Multiplying both sides by
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
, we obtain (
127
).
∎

4.3.1.
Total horizontality (modulo
\Delta
-exact terms) on
\mathrm{FC}\times\mathrm{FC}

One can summarize the results of Propositions
4.11
,
4.1
and Corollary

4.9

as follows. One has splitting of the tangent bundle of
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
into a direct sum of three integrable distributions

(131)

T\mathcal{U}=T^{I}\oplus T^{II}\oplus T^{III}.

Here:

•

T^{I}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\{(\chi,0)\;|\;\chi\in\mathrm{Harm^{1}_{A,A^{\prime}}}\}
– harmonic shifts of
A
.

•

T^{II}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\{(0,\gamma)\;|\;\gamma\in\Omega^{1}_{A^{\prime}\mathrm{-closed}}\}
– shifts of
A^{\prime}
.

•

T^{III}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\{(d_{A}\beta,d_{A^{\prime}}\beta)\;|\;\beta\in\Omega^{0}\}
– diagonal gauge transformations of
(A,A^{\prime})
.

For the bundle
\mathcal{D}
of formal half-densities on
H_{A}
over
\mathcal{U}
, one has three flat partial connections

\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}
(
120
),
\nabla_{A^{\prime}}=\delta_{A^{\prime}}
,
\nabla^{\mathrm{gauge}}
along these three distributions. Here
\nabla^{\mathrm{gauge}}
is such that its parallel transport takes a formal half-density
\psi(\mathsf{a})
over
(A,A^{\prime})
to the half-density
\psi(\mathrm{g}\mathsf{a}\mathrm{g}^{-1})
over
({}^{\mathrm{g}}A,{}^{\mathrm{g}}A^{\prime})
for any
\mathrm{g}\colon M\rightarrow G
.
These three partial connections can be assembled into a total flat connection

(132)

\nabla^{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}+\nabla_{A^{\prime}}+\nabla^{\mathrm{gauge}}.

The extended partition function then satisfies the total horizontality equation (modulo
\Delta
-exact terms):

(133)

\nabla^{\mathrm{tot}}Z=i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(r_{{\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}}}(\mathsf{a})Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\Big).

Here
\delta A^{\prime}
is replaced in
r
(as in Proposition
4.11
) with the expression

(134)

\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}=\delta A^{\prime}-d_{A^{\prime}}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\delta A

– a 1-form on
\mathcal{U}
valued in
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
which vanishes along
T^{I}
and
T^{III}
and coincides with
\delta A^{\prime}
on
T^{II}
. Put another way,
\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}
is the projector onto
T^{II}
in (
131
).

4.4.
Extension of
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}
to a horizontal nonhomogeneous form in
A^{\prime}

In this section we describe a refinement of Proposition
4.11
: one combines the partition function
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
with the BV generator in the r.h.s. of (
127
) into a nonhomogeneous form in
A^{\prime}

\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})+r(\mathsf{a})Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})+\underbrace{\cdots}_{\mathrm{degree}\;\geq 2\;\mathrm{in}\;A^{\prime}}

so that the full object satisfies horizontality condition

(135)

\widehat{\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=0

with respect to the flat partial superconnection
40
40

40

For the definition of a superconnection, see e.g.
[
Igu09
, Definition 1.2]
.
The superconnection (
136
) can be thought of as a correction of the trivial superconnection
\delta_{A^{\prime}}
by
\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}
– a
0
-form in
A^{\prime}
.

(136)

\widehat{\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}=\delta_{A^{\prime}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}

in the direction of
A^{\prime}
in the bundle of formal half-densities in
H_{A}
over
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
.
In particular, in degree zero in
A^{\prime}
, (
135
) is the equation
\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=0
(the BV quantum master equation) and in degree one in
A^{\prime}
, it yields the equation (
127
).

We proceed to the detailed construction.

Consider the following quadruple of nonhomogeneous forms in
A^{\prime}
valued in linear maps:

(137)

\displaystyle\widehat{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}})^{k}i_{A,A^{\prime}}=i+\mathbb{I}i+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Hom}(H_{A}^{\bullet},\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}))),

\displaystyle\widehat{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{A,A^{\prime}}(-\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}})^{k}=p+p\mathbb{P}+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Hom}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),H_{A}^{\bullet})),

\displaystyle\widehat{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}K_{A,A^{\prime}}(-\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}})^{k}=K+\Lambda+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}))),

\displaystyle\widehat{\Theta}

\displaystyle=p_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}(-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}})i_{A,A^{\prime}}\quad\in\Omega^{0,2}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(H^{\bullet}_{A})).

Here for the first three maps, the 0-form component in
A^{\prime}
is given by the usual maps
i,p,K
in
(A,A^{\prime})
-gauge and the 1-form component is given by the maps
\mathbb{I}i
,
p\mathbb{P}
,
\Lambda
, with
\mathbb{I},\mathbb{P},\Lambda
as in (
125
). In
\Omega^{i,j}(\mathcal{U})
,
(i,j)
is the form bi-degree along the two factors in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
.

We remark that sums in (
137
) are finite (stop at
k=2
)
since each factor
\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}
drops the form degree on
M
by one.

The triple
(\widehat{i},\widehat{p},\widehat{K})
above can be thought of as a promotion of the
(i,p,K)
triple (SDR data) associated to the
(A,A^{\prime})
-gauge-fixing to a
differential family
over
A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, cf. Lemma
4.14
below.

Definition 4.12
.

We define the extended desynchronized Chern-Simons perturbative partition function as

(138)

\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle}\;\exp\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\\
\in\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H_{A}[1]))

where
\Gamma
runs over connected graphs with

l\geq 0
loops and Feynman weights of graphs are defined as in (
95
), where we replace
K
with
\widehat{K}
and
i
with
\widehat{i}
.

Theorem 4.13
.

We have the horizontality relation

(139)

\widehat{\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=0,

with
\widehat{\nabla}_{A^{\prime}}
as in (
136
).

An alternative name for equation (
139
) is the
differential quantum master equation
, cf.
[
BCM12
]
.

The proof is based on the fact that the triple
(\widehat{i},\widehat{p},\widehat{K})
satisfies the the relations of an
(i,p,K)
triple,
with the differential
d_{A}
replaced by the total differential
\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A}
. Interestingly, in these relations cohomology
H_{A}
as a family over
A^{\prime}
attains a nontrivial total differential
41
41

41

Or: flat (partial) superconnection.

\delta_{A^{\prime}}+\widehat{\Theta}
, with
\widehat{\Theta}
of mixed degree along
\mathcal{U}
and along
H_{A}
but of total degree one.

Lemma 4.14
.

The triple
(\widehat{i},\widehat{p},\widehat{K})
satisfies the following relations:

(140a)

\displaystyle(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+[d_{A},-])\widehat{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\widehat{i}\,\widehat{p},

(140b)

\displaystyle(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A})\widehat{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{i}\,\widehat{\Theta},

(140c)

\displaystyle\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{p}-\widehat{p}\,d_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\widehat{\Theta}\,\widehat{p},

(140d)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}\,\widehat{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(140e)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}\,\widehat{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(140f)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(140g)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}\,\widehat{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

Formulae (
137
) and Lemma
4.14
are an application of homological perturbation lemma, see Appendix
C
.

Remark 4.15
.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.14
we have that
\widehat{\Theta}
satisfies

(141)

(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+\widehat{\Theta})^{2}=0,

or, equivalently,
\widehat{\Theta}
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

(142)

\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{\Theta}+\widehat{\Theta}^{2}=0.

Indeed, one has

\widehat{\Theta}^{2}\underset{(\ref{ipk hat pi=1})}{=}\widehat{p}\widehat{i}\widehat{\Theta}\widehat{\Theta}\underset{(\ref{ipK hat b})}{=}\widehat{p}\big((\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A})\widehat{i}\big)\widehat{\Theta}=\widehat{p}(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A})(\widehat{i}\,\widehat{\Theta})-\underbrace{\widehat{p}\,\widehat{i}}_{1}\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{\Theta}\\
\underset{(\ref{ipK hat b})}{=}\widehat{p}\underbrace{(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A})^{2}}_{0}\widehat{i}-\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{\Theta}=-\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{\Theta}.

Proof of Theorem
4.13
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.11
.

Consider the Feynman graph part of
\widehat{Z}
,
\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
, with
\Gamma
running over possibly disconnected graphs, with edges decorated by
\widehat{K}
and leaves decorated by
\widehat{i}(\mathsf{a})
. (We include powers of
\hbar
and the symmetry factor in
\widehat{\Phi}
.) The action of
\delta_{A^{\prime}}
on
\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
can be computed as a sum of (a) decorations of one edge of
\Gamma
with
(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+[d_{A},-])\widehat{K}=1-\widehat{i}\,\widehat{p}
(
140a
), plus (b) decorations of one leaf of
\Gamma
with
(\delta_{A^{\prime}}+d_{A})\widehat{i}=\widehat{i}\widehat{\Theta}
(
140b
).
Upon summing over graphs, contributions of
1
cancel out; contributions of

\widehat{i}\widehat{p}
yield
-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
; contributions of
\widehat{i}\widehat{\Theta}
add up to

-\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle,\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}\Big\}.

Thus, we have

(\delta_{A^{\prime}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}=-\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle,\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}\Big\}.

Next, we have

(\delta_{A^{\prime}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\Big(e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle}\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}\Big)=\\
=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle}\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},(\delta_{A^{\prime}}\widehat{\Theta}+\widehat{\Theta}^{2})\mathsf{a}\rangle+\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle,-\Big\}-\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle,-\Big\}\Big)\sum_{\Gamma}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma}=0,

where we used (
142
).
This proves the horizontality equation (
139
).
∎

4.4.1.
A path integral formula for
\widehat{Z}

The extended partition function (
138
)
can be seen as a perturbative expansion of the following path integral:

(143)

\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\int_{\mathcal{L}=\Omega_{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{-ex}}[1]}\mathcal{D}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(S_{CS}(A+i(\mathsf{a})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})+\\
+\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\big\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\,\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\big\rangle}_{\tau}+\underbrace{\big\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\,\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}i(\mathsf{a})\big\rangle}_{\sigma}\Big)

where we suppress the indices in
i_{A,A^{\prime}},G_{A,A^{\prime}}
. The addition of the second and third terms in the exponential generates Feynman diagrams with edges and leaves decorated by
\widehat{K}
and
\widehat{i}
instead of
K
and
i
. Additionally, one has a diagram consisting of two source terms
\sigma
connected by an edge
– this accounts for the exponential prefactor containing
\widehat{\Theta}
in
(
138
).
See Figure
8
.

Figure 8
.
Feynman diagrams containing vertices
\tau
and
\sigma
. Black dots are the usual internal vertices of Chern-Simons graphs.

4.5.
Metric dependence of the desynchronized partition function

Our definition of the desynchronized partition function
Z_{A,A^{\prime}}
(and its extended version) rely on a Riemannian metric
g
on
M
. In this subsection, we analyze the dependence of
Z
on this metric. Changing the metric induces another deformation of the gauge-fixing operator
d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}
. The goal of this subsection is to prove Theorem
4.17
below. Most of the discussion is analogous to the previous subsection and we only sketch the proofs.

Remark 4.16

(Framing anomaly and renormalization)
.

It is well known that the (synchronized) perturbative Chern-Simons partition function exhibits metric dependence known as
framing anomaly
. Namely, the phase
e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\psi_{A}}
of the synchronized 1-loop part

I_{A}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau^{\frac{1}{2}}_{A}

depends on the metric through the eta invariant
\psi
, as already discussed in Witten’s treatment
[
Wit89
]
.
42
42

42

Ray-Singer torsion, as an element of the determinant line
\mathrm{Det}\,H_{A}^{\bullet}
, is invariant under changes of metric (this result does not require the flat connection to be acyclic).

The dependence on the metric can be canceled by choosing a framing or 2-framing
\phi
of
M
,i.e. a trivialization of
TM
or
TM\oplus TM
43
43

43

Working with 2-framings has the advantage that 3-manifolds have a canonical 2-framing
[
Ati90
]
. Choosing this canonical 2-framing both simplifies the 1-loop part and conjecturally agrees with asymptotics of WRT invariants, see
[
FG91
]
.
, and multiplying
I_{A,A}
by
e^{\frac{i\dim G}{24}\cdot\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}
, where

S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)=S_{CS}(\phi^{*}A_{g})
denotes the evaluation of the Chern-Simons action on Levi-Civita connection
A_{g}
. For a 2-framing
\phi
, one defines
S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)=\frac{1}{2}S_{CS}(A_{g}\oplus A_{g})
. Then, one has that

(144)

I_{A}^{\mathrm{ren}}:=e^{\frac{i\dim G}{24}\cdot\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}I_{A}

is invariant under variations of the metric. Axelrod and Singer
[
AS91
]
,
[
AS94
]
showed that the anomaly persists at
even loop orders: boundary strata of the compactified configuration spaces corresponding to the collapse of all vertices of a connected component of a Feynman graph yield potentially non-zero contributions. However, one can show that there exists a power series

(145)

c(\hbar)=\frac{\dim G}{24}\hbar-\frac{h^{\vee}\dim G}{24\cdot(2\pi)}\hbar^{2}+O(\hbar^{4})\in\hbar\mathbb{R}[[\hbar]]

such that the renormalized perturbative partition function

(146)

Z^{\mathrm{ren}}_{A}:=Z_{A}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}

is independent of the metric
g
, up to an explicit BV exact term (also for
A
non-smooth), see
[
CM08
]
,
[
Mne19
]
,
[
Wer19
]
. Here
h^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
. Comparison with the non-perturbative answer suggests that all higher order terms in (
145
) vanish, see e.g. the discussion below Eq. (6.124) in
[
AS91
]
.
In our desynchronized setting, the same anomalies appear, and they can be canceled in the same way.

Let us now consider the effect of an infinitesimal deformation
g\mapsto g+\delta g
. We have

(147)

\delta_{g}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}=[d_{A^{\prime}}^{*},\lambda_{\delta g}]

where
\lambda_{\delta g}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathrm{Met},\operatorname{End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M))
is given by (see Lemma
B.7
)

(148)

\lambda_{\delta g}=\star^{-1}\delta_{g}\star=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}g^{-1}\delta g-\iota_{g^{-1}\delta_{g}}.

We note that we have

(149)

\delta_{g}\lambda_{\delta g}=-\star^{-1}(\delta_{g}\star)\star^{-1}\delta_{g}=-\lambda_{\delta_{g}}^{2}.

The
(i,p,K)
triple transforms as

(150)

\displaystyle\delta_{g}i_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-d_{A}\mathbb{I}_{\delta g}i_{A,A^{\prime}},

(151)

\displaystyle\delta_{g}p_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-p_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}_{\delta g}d_{A},

(152)

\displaystyle\delta_{g}K_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=[d_{A},\Lambda_{\delta g}]+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}_{\delta g}+\mathbb{I}_{\delta g}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

where

(153)

\Lambda_{\delta g}=K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda_{\delta g}K_{A,A^{\prime}},\qquad\mathbb{I}_{\delta g}=K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda_{\delta g},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{\delta g}=\lambda_{\delta g}K_{A,A^{\prime}}

are the endomorphisms of
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
analogous to
\Lambda,\mathbb{I},\mathbb{P}
defined in (
125
).

We then have the following theorem:

Theorem 4.17
.

For
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{FC}
a pair of close flat connections, we have

(154)

\delta_{g}Z_{A,A^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ren}}(\mathsf{a})=i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\left(r_{\delta g}(\mathsf{a})Z^{\mathrm{ren}}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\right)

where
r_{\delta g}(\mathsf{a})
is given by the sum of connected Feynman diagrams with one edge marked by
\Lambda_{\delta g}
or one leaf marked by
\mathbb{I}_{\delta g}
.

Sketch of the proof.

This proof is analogous to the proof of Proposition
4.11
, using the fact that the renormalization cancels potential contributions from hidden boundary strata.
∎

4.5.1.
Extension of
Z
to a horizontal nonhomogeneous form in
g
.

One can perform a construction analogous to the one in Section
4.4
and extend the desynchronized partition function to a horizontal non-homogeneous differential form in the metric direction. This generalizes the construction of Axelrod and Singer
[
AS94
]
to the desynchronized case and arbitrary kinetic operators.
44
44

44

Axelrod and Singer always work under the assumption that
d_{A}
is acyclic.
Namely, one has the following analog of (
137
):

(155)

\displaystyle\widehat{i}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}(K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda_{\delta g})^{k}i_{A,A^{\prime}}=i+\mathbb{I}_{\delta g}i+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U}\times\mathrm{Met},\mathrm{Hom}(H_{A}^{\bullet},\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}))),

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{A,A^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\delta g}K_{A,A^{\prime}})^{k}=p+p\mathbb{P}_{\delta g}+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U}\times\mathrm{Met},\mathrm{Hom}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}),H_{A}^{\bullet})),

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}K_{A,A^{\prime}}(\lambda_{\delta g}K_{A,A^{\prime}})^{k}=K+\Lambda_{\delta g}+\cdots\quad\in\Omega^{0,0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U}\times\mathrm{Met},\mathrm{End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}))),

\displaystyle\widehat{\Theta}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=-p_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda_{\delta g}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda_{\delta g}i_{A,A^{\prime}}\quad\in\Omega^{0,0,2}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{End}(H^{\bullet}_{A})).

Again, we note that since
K
reduces the form degree along
M
, these sums are finite: The sum stops at
k=2
for
\widehat{i}_{\delta g}
,
\widehat{p}_{\delta g}
and
\widehat{K}_{\lambda_{\delta g}}
.

Remark 4.18
.

Another way to construct the triple
(\widehat{i}_{\delta g},\widehat{p}_{\delta g},\widehat{K}_{\delta g})
is to consider the operator
45
45

45

This is the construction used by Axelrod and Singer
[
AS94
]
.

(156)

\widehat{H}_{g}=[d_{A}+\delta_{g},d^{*}_{A^{\prime},g}]=\widehat{H}_{g}+(\delta_{g}d_{A^{\prime},g}^{*})=\widehat{H}_{g}+[\lambda_{\delta g},d^{*}_{A^{\prime},g}].

The operator
H_{g}+P_{A,A^{\prime},g}+[\lambda_{\delta g},d^{*}_{A^{\prime},g}]
is invertible and its Green’s function can be computed as

\widehat{G}=G-G[\lambda_{\delta g},d^{*}_{A^{\prime},g}]G+\ldots
.
Upon applying
d^{*}_{A^{\prime},g}
, one recovers (
284
). In particular,
\widehat{K}_{\delta g}
coincides, for
A=A^{\prime}
an acyclic flat connection, with the extended propagator of Axelrod and Singer
[
AS94
, Section 4]
.

Similarly to Lemma
4.14
we have:

Lemma 4.19
.

The triple
(\widehat{i}_{\delta g},\widehat{p}_{\delta g},\widehat{K}_{\delta g})
satisfies the following relations:

(157a)

\displaystyle(\delta_{g}+[d_{A},-])\widehat{K}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\widehat{i}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{p}_{\delta g},

(157b)

\displaystyle(\delta_{g}+d_{A})\widehat{i}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{i}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{\Theta}_{\delta g},

(157c)

\displaystyle\delta_{g}\widehat{p}_{\delta g}-\widehat{p}_{\delta g}\,d_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\widehat{\Theta}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{p}_{\delta g},

(157d)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{i}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(157e)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{K}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(157f)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}_{\delta g}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(157g)

\displaystyle\widehat{p}_{\delta g}\,\widehat{i}_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

This is a special case of Proposition
C.1
for
\mathbb{GF}=\mathrm{Met}
.
The extended partition function

(158)

\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime};\delta g}(\mathsf{a})=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A_{0})}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle}\;\exp\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\widehat{\Phi}_{\Gamma,A,A^{\prime};\delta g}(\mathsf{a})\\
\in\Omega^{0,0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U}\times\mathrm{Met},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H_{A}))

then satisfies the differential Master Equation

(159)

(\delta_{g}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime};\delta g}^{\mathrm{ren}}=0,

which one can prove analogously to Theorem
4.13
; the superscript “ren” means that we include the renormalization factor as in (
146
). Again, one can view
\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime};\delta g}
as the perturbative expansion of the path integral

(160)

\widehat{Z}_{A,A^{\prime};\delta g}(\mathsf{a})=\int_{\mathcal{L}=\Omega_{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{-ex}}[1]}\mathcal{D}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\;\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(S_{CS}(A+i(\mathsf{a})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})-\\
-\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},\lambda_{\delta g}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\big\rangle+\big\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},\lambda_{\delta g}i(\mathsf{a})\big\rangle\Big)

with the last two terms generating additional vertices that sum up to
K_{\delta g},i_{\delta g}
and
\Theta_{\delta g}
respectively.

4.6.
Partition function extended to a nonhomogeneous form on
\mathrm{FC}\times\mathrm{FC}\times\mathrm{Met}
.

4.6.1.
Connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}

In this section,
\mathrm{Met}
will denote the space of Riemannian metrics on
M
and
\underline{\mathcal{U}}=\{(A,A^{\prime},g)\}
will stand for a sufficiently
thin open neighborhood of
\mathrm{Diag}\times\mathrm{Met}
in
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{Met}
, where each fixed-
g
slice consists of pairs
(A,A^{\prime})
that are close w.r.t.
g
.

Let

(161)

H=H_{\delta A}+H_{\delta A^{\prime}}+H_{\delta g}\quad\in\Omega^{1}(\underline{\mathcal{U}},\mathrm{End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g}))),

where

(162a)

\displaystyle H_{\delta A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(K\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}dK+K\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}P+P\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}K\Big),

(162b)

\displaystyle H_{\delta A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Big(dG\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}Kd+dG\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}P+P\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}Gd\Big),

(162c)

\displaystyle H_{\delta g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dK\lambda_{\delta g}Kd+dK\lambda_{\delta g}P+P\lambda_{\delta g}Kd.

We are suppressing the subscripts in
d_{A},d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},P_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, let

(163)

\Psi=\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\langle B,H(B)\rangle\quad\in\Omega^{1}(\underline{\mathcal{U}})\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[1])^{*}.

Here
B\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[1]
is the field. Also, we consider the connection

(164)

\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}=\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}+H=\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}+\{\Psi,-\}_{B}

on the trivial bundle
\underline{\Omega}
over
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
with fiber
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[1]
. Here
\{,\}_{B}
stands for the Poisson bracket in the fiber and
\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}=\delta_{A}+\delta_{A^{\prime}}+\delta_{g}
is the de Rham differential on
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
.

We have the following.

Lemma 4.20
.

(a)

The connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
preserves the harmonic, exact and coexact subbundles in
\underline{\Omega}
.
46
46

46

Put another way:
the parallel transport of the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
along a path
(A_{t},A^{\prime}_{t},g_{t})
,
t\in[0,1]
maps the desynchronized
(A_{0},A^{\prime}_{0})
-Hodge decomposition

\Omega=\mathrm{Harm}_{A_{0},A^{\prime}_{0}}\oplus\mathrm{im}(d_{A_{0}})\oplus\mathrm{im}(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}_{0}})
(with metric
g_{0}
) to the desynchronized
(A_{1},A^{\prime}_{1})
-Hodge decomposition
\Omega=\mathrm{Harm}_{A_{1},A^{\prime}_{1}}\oplus\mathrm{im}(d_{A_{1}})\oplus\mathrm{im}(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}_{1}})
(with metric
g_{1}
) term-to-term.

(b)

\nabla
is symplectic, i.e., its parallel transport along any path in the base is a symplectomorphism between fibers w.r.t. the BV symplectic form
\omega=\int_{M}\langle-\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wedge}}{{,}}-\rangle
.

(c)

The curvature of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
is

(165)

F_{\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}}=P\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}(K\lambda_{\delta g}-G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}})P+P(\lambda_{\delta g}K-\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G)\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}P+\\
+d(K\lambda_{\delta g}-G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}})(Kd+P)\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}K+K\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}(dK+P)(\lambda_{\delta g}K-\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G)d.

In particular, the curvature has vanishing
(\delta A)^{2}
,
(\delta A^{\prime})^{2}
,
(\delta g)^{2}
and
\delta A^{\prime}\delta g
terms; only
\delta A\delta A^{\prime}
and
\delta A\delta g
terms are nonzero.

(d)

For a fixed metric
g
and restricted to harmonic forms,
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
coincides with
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
of Section
2.5.3
.

(Proven by an explicit computation.)

Remark 4.21
.
47
47

47

We thank S. Stolz for
this remark.

\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
can be seen as a sum of three “shift-and-project connections” (cf. Remark
2.31
) induced on the harmonic, exact and coexact subbundles in
\underline{\Omega}
from the trivial connection on
\underline{\Omega}
. Put another way, one can write

(166)

\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}=\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}-\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}(P)\,P-\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}(dK)\,dK-\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}(Kd)\,Kd.

Here
P,dK,Kd
are the fiberwise projections onto the three terms in the Hodge decomposition.

By restricting
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
to harmonic forms and projecting to cohomology
H_{A}
,
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
induces the connection
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}
on the cohomology bundle bundle
\mathbb{H}
over
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
with fiber
H_{A}
.
48
48

48

These objects are a natural extension of the corresponding objects of Section

2.5.4
by allowing variation of metric. By an abuse of notations, we use
\mathbb{H}
,
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}
for the extension.

Notice that the cohomology bundle is trivial along
\mathrm{GF}=\mathrm{FC}_{A^{\prime}}\times\mathrm{Met}
directions and the connection is also trivial in these directions, i.e.

(167)

\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}=\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}_{A}+\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}.

The curvature of
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}
corresponds to the harmonic-harmonic block of (
165
):

(168)

F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}=p\Big((\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}(K\lambda_{\delta g}-G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}))+(\lambda_{\delta g}K-\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\delta A^{\prime}}G)\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}\Big)i.

Furthermore, we will denote by
\nabla^{\mathcal{D}}
the connection induced by
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}
on the bundle
\mathcal{D}
of formal half-densities on
H_{A}
over
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
.

4.6.2.
Extended partition function

Denote
49
49

49

This term is completely analogous to the term in the extended action denoted
S_{R}
in
[
BCM12
]
and is the Hamiltonian (in an appropriate sense) for
the Grothendieck connection, hence the superscript
G
.

\Psi^{G}=\int_{M}\langle\delta A,B\rangle.

Let

(169)

\check{S}(B)=S_{CS}(A+B)-\Psi^{G}-\Psi\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\underline{\mathcal{U}})\otimes\mathrm{Sym}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})[1])^{*}.

– a form on
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
valued in polynomials in

. We split the field as

(170)

Perturbative evaluation of (

) yields the following:

(171)

\check{Z}(\mathsf{a})=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}(-\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\check{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle)}\cdot\\
\cdot\exp\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\check{\Phi}_{\Gamma}.

Here:

•

\Gamma
runs over connected trivalent graphs with leaves, as usual.

•

\check{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
is the Feynman weight of the graph
\Gamma
, where an edge is assigned the extended propagator

(172)

\check{K}=\sum_{k=0}^{2}K(HK)^{k}

– a nonhomogeneous form on
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
valued in

;
here

) of the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
.
A leaf is assigned the expression

\check{i}(\mathsf{a})=\sum_{k=0}^{2}(KH)^{k}i(\mathsf{a})+K\delta A.

Note that

is affine-linear in
\mathsf{a}
, rather than just linear. Both (

) and (

) stop at

because
