decreases form degree by 1.

•

\check{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})
stands for

(174)

\check{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})=-pHKHi(\mathsf{a}).

It is a 2-form on
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
with values in endomorphisms of
H_{A}[1]
.

4.6.3.
Differential quantum master equation

Theorem 4.23
.

The following differential quantum master equation holds:

(175)

\Big(\nabla^{\mathcal{D}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}-\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle\Big)(e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}\check{Z})=0,

with
F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}
as in (
168
).

Heuristic Path Integral Argument:

Denote
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{im}(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}})
the gauge fixing Lagrangian. We have

(176)

\nabla^{\mathcal{D}}\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}=\\
=\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}\frac{i}{\hbar}(\delta_{A}S_{CS}(A+B)-\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}\Psi+\{\check{S},\Psi\}_{B}-i\hbar\Delta_{B}\Psi).

Here in the brackets in the r.h.s., the first two terms account for
\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}
acting on the integrand and the last two terms account for the change of gauge-fixing induced by an infinitesimal change of
A,A^{\prime},g
, cf.
[
CM08
, Proposition 2]
.
Also, note that we can write the first term in the r.h.s. of (
176
) as

\delta_{A}S_{CS}(A+B)=\{S_{CS}(A+B),\Psi^{G}\}_{B}.

Next, applying the BV Laplacian in zero-modes to
\check{Z}
, we have, using the BV-Stokes’ theorem,

(177)

-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}-i\hbar\Delta_{B}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}=\\
=\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}\frac{i}{\hbar}(\frac{1}{2}\{\check{S},\check{S}\}_{B}-i\hbar\Delta_{B}\check{S})\\
=\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}\frac{i}{\hbar}(-\{S_{CS},\Psi^{G}\}_{B}-\{\check{S},\Psi\}_{B}-\frac{1}{2}\{\Psi,\Psi\}_{B}+i\hbar\Delta_{B}\Psi)\\
=\int_{\mathcal{L}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\check{S}(B)}\frac{i}{\hbar}(-\delta_{A}S_{CS}-\{\check{S},\Psi\}_{B}+\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}\Psi-\Psi^{F}+i\hbar\Delta_{B}\Psi).

In the last transition we used that

\delta^{\mathrm{tot}}\Psi+\frac{1}{2}\{\Psi,\Psi\}_{B}=\Psi^{F}
, with
\Psi^{F}=\frac{1}{2}\langle B,F_{\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}}B\rangle
– the quadratic form associated with the curvature (
165
) of
\nabla^{\mathrm{Hodge}}
. Note that
\Psi^{F}|_{B=i(\mathsf{a})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle
for
\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\in\mathcal{L}
.

Thus, (
176
) and (
177
) differ by

\check{Z}\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle
.

Sketch of diagrammatic proof of Theorem
4.23
.

For the purpose of the proof we expand the partition function
\check{Z}
as a sum over graphs with trivalent and univalent vertices and leaves. Edges are assigned the extended propagator
\check{K}=\sum_{k=0}^{2}K(HK)^{k}
, leaves the extended inclusion
\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}(\mathsf{a})=\sum_{k=0}^{2}(KH)^{k}i(\mathsf{a})
, and univalent vertices are assigned
\delta A
.
Notice that we have

(178)

\displaystyle\delta_{A}\check{K}

\displaystyle=\check{K}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}\check{K},

(179)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}_{A}\check{i}_{GF}

\displaystyle=\check{K}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta A}\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}},

(180)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{K}

\displaystyle=-[d,\check{K}]+\mathrm{id}-\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{p}_{\mathrm{GF}},

(181)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}

\displaystyle=-d\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}+\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{\Theta}.

Now the proof follows closely the proof of Theorem
4.13
. When computing
\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{Z}
, there are now additional terms when
\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}
hits the edge incident to a univalent vertex. Here, the terms
\mathrm{id}-\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{p}_{\mathrm{GF}}
survive. The first term is canceled by a graph with a
\delta A
leaf that is produced when applying
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}_{A}
. A special case occurs for the graph consisting of a graph with one univalent vertex, one trivalent vertex and two leaves, here one such term survives and is canceled by the curvature (
168
) and
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}_{A}\check{\Theta}
, see Figure
11
. The second term involving
\check{i}_{\mathrm{GF}}\check{p}_{\mathrm{GF}}
(which applied to
\delta A
is simply
P
) is canceled by a term in
\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}(e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle}\Phi_{\Gamma}(\mathsf{a}))
.

\delta_{\mathrm{GF}}
K
\mathsf{a}
\mathsf{a}
=
\mathsf{a}
\mathsf{a}
\delta A
+\ldots

Figure 11
.
The graph on the right evaluates to
\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla}(\mathsf{a})\rangle+\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}_{A}\check{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})
, with
F_{\nabla}
given in (
168
). Thick edge stands for
\check{i}
, thin edges stand for
K
(between vertices) or
i
(at leaves).

As always, there are extra terms in the metric dependence of the partition function due to the total collapse of a connected component of a Feynman graph (canceled by
S_{\mathrm{grav}}
counterterm). Incorporating that, we get (
175
).
∎

Remark 4.24
.

Note that while
\nabla^{\mathcal{D}}
is not flat, the superconnection

(182)

\check{\nabla}=\nabla^{\mathcal{D}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}-\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle

on the bundle of formal half-densities on
H_{A}
, appearing in (
175
), is flat. In more detail: one has

(183)

(\nabla^{\mathcal{D}})^{2}=\langle F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}(\mathsf{a}),\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Str}_{H_{A}[1]}F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}

and

(184)

\check{\nabla}^{2}=(\nabla^{\mathcal{D}})^{2}+\underbrace{[\nabla^{\mathcal{D}},-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}]}_{0}-\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{[\nabla^{\mathcal{D}},\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle]}_{=\langle\mathsf{a},(\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}})\mathsf{a}\rangle=0\,\mathrm{by\,Bianchi\,identity}}+\\
+\underbrace{(-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})^{2}}_{0}-[\Delta_{\mathsf{a}},\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle]+\underbrace{(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle)^{2}}_{0}\\
=(\nabla^{\mathcal{D}})^{2}+\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle,-\Big\}_{\mathsf{a}}-\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},F_{\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}}\mathsf{a}\rangle\Big)\underset{(\ref{nabla^D squared})}{=}0.

4.6.4.
Partial extensions of
Z
along
A
,
A^{\prime}
and
g
– comparison with previous results

Varying
A^{\prime}
.

If we consider the slice of
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
with fixed
A,g
and varying
A^{\prime}
,
the path integral (
170
) reduces to (
143
). The corresponding restriction of (
175
) to
\delta A=\delta g=0
is equivalent to (
139
). Also, in this case
\check{K},\check{i},\check{\Theta}
reduce to
\widehat{K},\widehat{i}
and
\widehat{\Theta}
of Section
4.4
.

Varying
g
.

Similarly, if we consider the slice of
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
with fixed
A,A^{\prime}
and varying
g
,

\check{Z}
reduces to (
160
), (
158
); dQME (
175
) becomes (
159
);
\check{K},\check{i},\check{\Theta}
become
\widehat{K}_{\delta g},\widehat{i}_{\delta g}
and
\widehat{\Theta}_{\delta g}
of Section
4.5.1
, respectively.

Varying
A
.

One can also consider varying
A
while keeping
A^{\prime},g
fixed.
In this case (
171
) simplifies to

(185)

\check{Z}_{\delta A}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle}\exp\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{l(\Gamma)-1}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\check{\Phi}_{\Gamma}

where in
\check{\Phi}_{\Gamma}
, edges are decorated with the usual non-extended propagator
K
and leaves are decorated with
i(\mathsf{a})-K\delta A
. By (
175
),
\check{Z}_{\delta A}
satisfies the dQME in the direction of shifts of
A
:

(186)

(\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\check{Z}_{\delta A}=0

or, equivalently,

(187)

(\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}-\langle[\delta A],\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\rangle+\langle p\,\mathrm{ad}_{K\delta A}i(\mathsf{a}),\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}}\rangle-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\widetilde{Z}_{\delta A}=0

where
\widetilde{Z}_{\delta A}
is (
185
) with two modifications: (i) factor
e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle}
is removed, (ii) the graph consisting of the single cubic vertex is removed from the sum over
\Gamma
.

We note that the result (
187
) restricted to degree
1
in
\delta A
is equivalent to (
133
) restricted to
\delta A^{\prime}=0
as one has, from inspection of Feynman diagrams,

(188)

\widetilde{Z}_{\delta A}=Z+r_{\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}=-d_{A^{\prime}}K\delta A}Z+\underbrace{\cdots}_{\mathrm{degree}\;\geq 2\;\mathrm{in}\;\delta A},

with
r
and
\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}
as in (
133
), (
134
).
50
50

50

This is a consequence of the following observations: (i) an edge with a
K\delta A
leaf plugged in contributes to
\check{\Phi}
as
K\mathrm{ad}_{K\delta A}K=K[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{K\delta A}]G=K\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{d_{A^{\prime}}K\delta A}G=\Lambda_{\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}}
; (ii) a
K\delta A
leaf joining an
i(\mathsf{a})
leaf and continuing with an edge contributes
K\mathrm{ad}_{K\delta A}i(\mathsf{a})=G[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{K\delta A}]i(\mathsf{a})=G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{d_{A^{\prime}}K\delta A}i(\mathsf{a})=\mathbb{I}_{\widetilde{\delta A^{\prime}}}i(\mathsf{a})
. Thus, allowing one
K\delta A
leaf in a graph results in graphs of
rZ
. Here
\Lambda
and
\mathbb{I}
are as in (
125
).

To elucidate this equivalence, we note for harmonic shifts
\delta A
, the first two terms in (
187
) yield the connection
\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}
(
120
); for
\delta A
exact, there is a discrepancy between
\nabla^{\mathbb{H},A^{\prime}}
and
\nabla^{\mathrm{tot}}
(
132
) compensated by the third term in (
187
).
51
51

51

This discrepancy arises from the fact that the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
, for an infinitesimal exact shift
\delta A=d\beta
(and
\delta A^{\prime}=0
), shifts a harmonic form
\chi
to
\chi-K\mathrm{ad}_{d\beta}\chi=\chi-\mathrm{ad}_{\beta}\chi+dK\mathrm{ad}_{\beta}\chi+P\mathrm{ad}_{\beta}\chi
. Here
\mathrm{ad}_{\beta}\chi
corresponds to
\nabla^{\mathrm{gauge}}+\nabla_{A^{\prime}}
(Section
4.3.1
), the term
d(\cdots)
is irrelevant in cohomology and
P\mathrm{ad}_{\beta}\chi=P\mathrm{ad}_{K\delta A}\chi
is the discrepancy.

5.
The global partition function

In this section we will discuss the properties of the “synchronized” partition function
Z(A_{0},\mathsf{a})=Z_{A_{0},A_{0}}(\mathsf{a})
defined in Definition
3.1
seen as a family
\underline{Z}
over the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. We introduce the
global partition function

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
– a volume form on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
arising from modifying
\underline{Z}
to a global (
\nabla^{G}
-horizontal) object by a BV-exact term and then restricting it to
\mathsf{a}=0
. Finally, we study the dependence of
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
on metric.

5.1.
Perturbative partition function
\underline{Z}
on the moduli space

5.1.1.
Bundles over
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

We recall that
\mathrm{FC}
denotes the space of all flat connections,
\mathcal{G}=C^{\infty}(M,G)
the gauge group and

(189)

\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{FC}/\mathcal{G}

the moduli space of flat connections, with
\pi\colon\mathrm{FC}\to\mathcal{M}
the projection. Consider the subsets

(190)

\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{FC},\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}

of smooth irreducible points, by the results of Section
2
, they are smooth manifolds.
Over
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, we have the cohomology bundle
\mathbb{H}\to\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, with fiber over
A_{0}
given by
H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}
. The gauge group acts on
\mathbb{H}
by conjugation, we have
{}^{\mathrm{g}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})=H_{{}^{\mathrm{g}}A_{0}}
. The quotient

\mathbb{H}[1]/\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the bundle
T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\oplus T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

5.1.2.
Perturbative partition function

Restricted to
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
, the perturbative partition function
Z
defined in (
94
) defines a section

(191)

Z\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(-)}\Gamma(\mathrm{FC}^{\prime},\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}\,\mathbb{H}[1]^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{H})[[\hbar]].

From
[
CM08
]
we have the following result.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
A_{0}
is an irreducible flat connection. Then

Z_{A_{0}}(\mathsf{a})\in\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}\,H^{1}_{A_{0}}\otimes\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}})[[\hbar]]

depends only on the 1-form part
\mathsf{a}^{1}
of
\mathsf{a}
.

Finally, we have the following:

Proposition 5.2
.

The Chern-Simons partition function
Z
is equivariant with respect to the action of the gauge group on
\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from Proposition
4.1
by restricting to the diagonal
A=A^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

The perturbative partition function defines a section

(192)

\underline{Z}\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(-)}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\widehat{\operatorname{Sym}}\,T^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\otimes\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\oplus T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{*})[[\hbar]].

Proof.

This follows from (
191
) together with Lemma
5.1
and Proposition
5.2
(notice that the quotient bundle
\mathbb{H}^{1}[1]/\mathcal{G}\cong T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.)
∎

5.1.3.
Naive global partition function

Restricting
Z,\underline{Z}
to
\mathsf{a}=0
, we obtain the naive global partition functions

(193)

\displaystyle Z^{\mathrm{glob,naive}}_{A}

\displaystyle=Z_{A}(0)\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}\Gamma(\mathrm{FC}^{\prime},\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{H})[[\hbar]],

\displaystyle\underline{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,naive}}_{A}

\displaystyle=\underline{Z}_{A}(0)\in e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\oplus T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{*})[[\hbar]].

Remark 5.4
.

The bundle
\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\oplus T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{*}
is canonically isomorphic to the bundle of top forms
\operatorname{Det}(T^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
over
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. From the BV viewpoint, it is also natural to identify this bundle with the subbundle of half-densities on
T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
which do not depend on the fiber coordinates.

In Section
52
below we will construct the non-naive global partition function – a modification of
\underline{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,naive}}
yielding an invariant of
M
as a framed 3-manifold (Theorem
5.22
).

5.2.
Almost horizontality of
\underline{Z}
w.r.t. Grothendieck connection

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\underline{\varphi}\colon U\subset T\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be the sum-over-trees exponential map (
69
) induced by the SDR
(i_{A},p_{A},K_{A})
. Fix
A
and small
\alpha\in H^{1}_{A}
, and let
B=d_{\alpha}\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\alpha)\colon H^{1}_{A}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\alpha)}
. Then, for
\mathsf{a}\in H^{1}_{A}
small, we have

(194)

\det(B^{\vee})\circ\underline{Z}_{\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\alpha)}(B(\mathsf{a}))=\underline{Z}_{A}(\alpha+\mathsf{a})+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}R(A,\alpha,\mathsf{a}),

where

(195)

R(A,\alpha,\mathsf{a})=\det(B^{\vee})\circ R_{\widetilde{A},A,\widetilde{A}}(\mathfrak{B}(\mathsf{a}))\\
=\int_{0}^{1}dt\,r_{\widetilde{A},A_{t};\dot{A}_{t}}(\mathfrak{B}(\mathsf{a}))\cdot\det(B^{\vee})\circ Z_{\widetilde{A},A_{t}}(B(\mathsf{a})).

Here:
R_{\widetilde{A},A,\widetilde{A}}
is as in (
128
),

\widetilde{A}=\varphi_{A,A}(\alpha)
(hence
[\widetilde{A}]=\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\alpha)
),
A_{t}=\varphi_{A,A}(t\alpha)
is a path from
A
to
\widetilde{A}
,
\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A,A}\colon H^{\bullet}_{A}\rightarrow H^{\bullet}_{\widetilde{A}}
is the promotion of
B
to a map between full cohomology,
r
is as in Proposition
4.11
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Theorems
4.3
and
4.10
.
Indeed, we have

(196)

\det(B^{\vee})\circ\underline{Z}_{\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\alpha)}(B(\mathsf{a}))=\det(B^{\vee})\circ Z_{\widetilde{A},\widetilde{A}}(B(\mathsf{a}))\\
\underset{\mathrm{Theorem\,}\ref{thm: change gf}}{=}\det(B^{\vee})\circ\Big(Z_{\widetilde{A},A}(B(\mathsf{a}))+i\hbar(\Delta_{\mathsf{b}}R_{\widetilde{A},A,\widetilde{A}}(\mathsf{b}))\Big|_{\mathsf{b}=B(\mathsf{a})}\Big)\\
\underset{\mathrm{Theorem\,}\ref{thm 4.2}}{=}Z_{A,A}(\alpha+\mathsf{a})+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\Big(\det(B^{\vee})\circ R_{\widetilde{A},A,\widetilde{A}}(\mathfrak{B}(\mathsf{a}))\Big).

∎

By taking the derivative of (
194
) in
\alpha
at
\alpha=0
one obtains the following.

Corollary 5.6
.

The partition function
\underline{Z}
is horizontal w.r.t. Grothendieck connection modulo a BV-exact term:

(197)

\nabla^{G}\underline{Z}=i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\left(r_{A,A;\delta A}(\mathsf{a})Z_{A,A}(\mathsf{a})\right),

with
r
as in Proposition
4.11
.

5.3.
Extended smoothness assumption

Consider the tree-level expression appearing in the extended partition function (
138
)

(198)

\Xi=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathsf{a},\widehat{\Theta}(\mathsf{a})\rangle+\sum_{T}\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(T)|}\widehat{\Phi}_{T,A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\in\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\mathcal{U},\mathrm{Sym}(H_{A}[1])^{*})

with
T
running over binary rooted trees up to isomorphism; the notations are as in (
138
).

Definition 5.7
.

We will say that
(A,A^{\prime})\in\mathcal{U}
satisfies the
1-extended smoothness assumption
if the
(0,1)
-form component of
\Xi
vanishes, i.e., if the sum of trees with one edge marked by
\Lambda
or one leaf marked by
\mathbb{I}i
or the root marked by
p\mathbb{P}
(see (
125
)) is zero. Note that the
(0,0)
-form component of
\Xi
vanishes by the usual smoothness assumption on
A
. Furthermore, we will say that
(A,A^{\prime})
satisfies the
fully extended smoothness assumption
if
\Xi=0
.

1-extended smoothness assumption will be useful for our applications (reduction of the formal exponential map and of the Grothendieck connection to the moduli space), while the fully extended one is given as a natural refinement.

Remark 5.8
.

Interpretation of 1-extended smoothness assumption: a variation of
A^{\prime}
induces a variation of the Hodge SDR data
(i,p,K)
, which in turn induces an
L_{\infty}
automorphism of the
L_{\infty}
algebra structure on
H_{A}^{\bullet}
(with vanishing operations, as per the usual smoothness assumption). The 1-extended smoothness assumption asks that this
L_{\infty}
automorphism is trivial as well.

Remark 5.9
.

Fully extended smoothness assumption can be interpreted using the construction of Appendix
C
as follows: we are considering the dg Lie algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{GF}),d_{A}+\delta_{A^{\prime}},[,]
, with
\mathbb{GF}
as in (
286
), and we are asking that the homotopy transfer to
H^{\bullet}_{A}\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{GF})
yields an
L_{\infty}
algebra structure with
l_{1}=\delta_{A^{\prime}}
and all other operations vanishing.

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider a path
(A,A^{\prime}_{t})
in
\mathcal{U}
. Then, under 1-extended smoothness assumption,
the sum-over-trees map
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}

satisfies

(199)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})=d_{\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})}\gamma

for sufficiently small
\mathsf{a}
.
Here
\gamma
is given by the sum over trees with
K
on the root and either one edge (or the root) marked by
\Lambda_{\dot{A}^{\prime}_{t}}
or one leaf marked by
\mathbb{I}_{\dot{A}^{\prime}_{t}}i(\mathsf{a})
.

In particular, as
t
changes,
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})
changes by a gauge transformation.

Sketch of proof.

We have
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})=A+\delta_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})
, with
\delta
being the sum over trees with
K
on the root. Therefore, the l.h.s. of (
199
) is the sum over trees with one edge (or the root) marked by
[d,\Lambda]
or one leaf marked by
d\mathbb{I}i
(we suppress the subscripts, in particular,
d=d_{A}
). As in the proof of Proposition
4.11
, using the Stokes’ theorem on the configuration space to move
d
from the marked edge or leaf to other edges, we obtain a sum of:

(1)

Trees with one edge (or root) marked by
\Lambda
or one leaf marked by
\mathbb{I}i
and one other edge marked by
[d,K]=1-P
.

(2)

Trees with one edge marked by
\Lambda
or one leaf marked by
\mathbb{I}i
and the root marked by
Kd=1-P-dK
.

(3)

Trees with the root marked by
d\Lambda
.

Contributions of 1 on the edge from (1) cancel out in the sum over graphs by the classical master equation (IHX relation). Contributions of
P
on the edge from (1) and (2) cancel out: (a) if the subtree between
P
and the leaves does not contain
\mathbb{I},\Lambda
, such subtrees add up to zero by smoothness; (b) if the subtree does contain
\mathbb{I}
or
\Lambda
, then such subtrees add up to zero by 1-extended smoothness. The remaining contributions are:
dK
from (2) and
d\Lambda
from (3) on the root add up to
d\gamma
;
1
from (2) yields
[\delta_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a}),\gamma]
(from pairs of trees joined at the root, one tree containing
\Lambda
or
\mathbb{I}
and one not). Thus, we obtain

(200)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})=d\gamma+[\delta_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a}),\gamma]=d_{\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}(\mathsf{a})}\gamma.

∎

In the remainder of the current subsection we will always be assuming 1-extended smoothness.

Let

(201)

\pi\colon\mathrm{FC}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}

be the quotient map sending a flat connection to its gauge equivalence class.

Corollary 5.11
.

We have that

(202)

\pi\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})=\colon\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\mathsf{a})

is independent of
A^{\prime}
close to
A
and agrees with the formal exponential map (
69
) on the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
induced from synchronized Hodge gauge-fixing
(i_{A,A},p_{A,A},K_{A,A})
.

Proof.

Obvious from Proposition
5.10
. For the second part, set
A^{\prime}=A
.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

The cohomology comparison map

(203)

\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})\leftarrow A,A^{\prime}}\colon H_{A}\rightarrow H_{\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})}

is independent of
A^{\prime}
and coincides with
d_{\mathsf{a}}\underline{\varphi}_{A}
.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
5.11
by differentiating (
202
).
∎

Next, consider the setting of Remark
4.8
and set
A^{\prime}=A
. We have

(204)

\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\mathsf{a})=\pi\varphi_{A,A}(\mathsf{a})\underset{(\ref{rem: partial Grothendieck connection eq1})}{=}\pi\varphi_{\widetilde{A},A}(\widetilde{\mathsf{a}})\underset{\mathrm{Cor.}\;\ref{cor 5.9}}{=}\pi\varphi_{\widetilde{A},\widetilde{A}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{a}})=\underline{\varphi}_{\widetilde{A}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{a}})

with
\widetilde{A}=\varphi_{A,A}(\alpha)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{a}}=\mathfrak{B}_{\widetilde{A}\leftarrow A,A}(\mathsf{a}-\alpha)
. Comparing with (
71
), we have the following.

Corollary 5.13
.

The restriction of the partial Grothendieck connection
\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}
(
120
) to the diagonal
A^{\prime}=A
upon reduction to the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
agrees with the Grothendieck connection
\nabla^{G}
(
71
) associated with the synchronized Hodge gauge-fixing. More precisely:
the parallel transport of
\widetilde{\nabla}^{G}
from
(A,A)
to
(\widetilde{A},A)
coincides with the parallel transport of
\nabla^{G}
from
[A]
to
[\widetilde{A}]
, with
\widetilde{A}=\varphi_{A,A}(\alpha)
and
\alpha\in H^{1}_{A}
small.

Corollary 5.14
.

The curvature of the connections
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
and
\nabla^{\mathbb{H}}
in the bundle of harmonic forms and the cohomology bundle over
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}\times\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}
vanishes when restricted to
\delta A
harmonic (with
\delta A^{\prime}
arbitrary).

Proof.

The curvature (
85
) with
\delta A
harmonic coincides with

O(\delta A\delta A^{\prime})
term in
the contribution of the cubic corolla graph in
\Xi
(
198
), evaluated on
\mathsf{a}+[\delta A]
, which vanishes by 1-extended smoothness assumption.
∎

Definition 5.15
.

We will say that
(A,A^{\prime},g)\in\underline{\mathcal{U}}
satisfies
metric-extended smoothness
if it satisfies the following two properties:

(a)

1-extended smoothness in the sense of Definition
5.7
;

(b)

the contribution of tree graphs in
r_{\delta g}(\mathsf{a})
in Theorem
4.17
vanishes.

Metric-extended smoothness implies the obvious generalization of the statements in this section to variations of metric, in particular:

•

The gauge class of
\varphi_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathsf{a})
is independent of
g
and yields
\underline{\varphi}_{A}(\mathsf{a})
. In particular, the latter exponential map on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
does not depend on
g
. Hence, also the Grothendieck connection
\nabla^{G}
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, associated with
\underline{\varphi}
, does not depend on
g
.

5.4.
Correcting
\underline{Z}
to a global object. Definition of

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
.

52
52

52

Main statements of this section are a Chern-Simons counterpart of Theorem 6.1 in
[
BCM12
]
.

Throughout this subsection we are assuming 1-extended smoothness.

Theorem 5.16
.

\underline{Z}
can be modified by a BV-exact term (pointwise on the moduli space) to a global object. I.e., there exists a degree
-1
element
\rho\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1]))
such that

(205)

Z^{\mathrm{mod}}\colon=\underline{Z}+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\rho

satisfies

(206)

\nabla^{G}Z^{\mathrm{mod}}=0.

Proof.

Step 1.

We extend
\underline{Z}
to a nonhomogeneous form on the moduli space,

(207)

\widetilde{Z}=\underline{Z}+\sum_{p\geq 1}R^{(p)}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H_{A}^{\bullet}[1])),

with
R^{(p)}
a
p
-form on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of ghost degree
-p
and
R^{(1)}
being the generator in the r.h.s. of (
197
), with the extension satisfying

(208)

(\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\widetilde{Z}=0.

Explicitly, we construct
\widetilde{Z}
as

(209)

\widetilde{Z}=\check{Z}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle}\Big|_{A^{\prime}=A,\delta A^{\prime}=\delta A\mathrm{\,harmonic},\;\delta g=0}=\widehat{Z}\Big|_{A^{\prime}=A,\delta A^{\prime}=\delta A\mathrm{\,harmonic}}

with
\check{Z}
as in (
171
) and
\widehat{Z}
as in (
138
), with the r.h.s. considered modulo gauge transformations. Then, assuming 1-extended smoothness, the dQME (
175
) yields (
208
).

Step 2.
(Building a chain contraction for
\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}
.)
The cohomology of the complex
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])),\nabla^{G}
is concentrated in form degree
0
and is isomorphic to global half-densities
\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
.
53
53

53

See
[
BCM12
, Section 2]
.

More precisely, one has SDR data
(\mathsf{i},\mathsf{p},\mathsf{K})
with inclusion
\mathsf{i}=T\underline{\varphi}^{*}
and projection
\mathsf{p}
given by evaluating a formal half-density at
\mathsf{a}^{1}=0
,

(210)

\mathsf{p}:\psi([A],\mathsf{a}^{1},\mathsf{a}^{2})D^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{a}^{1}D^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{a}^{2}\mapsto\psi([A],0,\mathsf{a}^{2})D^{\frac{1}{2}}[A]D^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{a}^{2}

where
\mathsf{a}^{1},\mathsf{a}^{2}
are the components of
\mathsf{a}
in
H^{1}_{A}=T_{[A]}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
H^{2}_{A}=T^{*}_{[A]}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. It is also understood that
\mathsf{p}
sends forms of positive degree on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to zero.

Next, deform the differential on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1]))
from
\nabla^{G}
to
\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}
. By homological perturbation lemma (Lemma
A.2
), one has deformed SDR data

(211)

(\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{p}^{\prime},\mathsf{K}^{\prime})\colon(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H^{\bullet}_{A}[1])),\nabla^{G}+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\leadsto(\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}),\delta).

Moreover, the fact that
\mathsf{K}
lowers form degree along
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
by one, implies that

•

the induced differential
\delta=-i\hbar\Delta
is the BV Laplacian on (global) half-densities
T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,

•

\mathsf{i}^{\prime}=\mathsf{i}
.

Step 3.
Using the fact that
K^{\prime}
defined above satisfies the chain homotopy property
\mathrm{id}=\mathsf{i}\mathsf{p}^{\prime}+[\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}},\mathsf{K}^{\prime}]
, we have

(212)

\widetilde{Z}=\mathsf{i}\mathsf{p}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z}+(\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z}+\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\underbrace{(\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\widetilde{Z}}_{=0\mathrm{\;by\;(\ref{dQME on ul til Z})}}.

Denote the first term on the r.h.s. by
Z^{\mathrm{mod}}\colon=\mathsf{i}\mathsf{p}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z}
. Since it is in the image of
\mathsf{i}
, it is
\nabla^{G}
-closed, and hence a global object. Restricting (
212
) to form degree zero along
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and denoting

(213)

\rho=(\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z})|_{\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\cdots)},

we obtain (
205
).
∎

Remark 5.17
.

Note that 1-extended smoothness implies
\rho=\underline{Z}\cdot O(\hbar^{0})
and
Z^{\mathrm{mod}}=\underline{Z}\cdot(1+O(\hbar))
.

Definition 5.18
.

We define the global partition function as the degree zero half-density on
T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(or, equivalently, a volume form on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
) given by restriction
Z^{\mathrm{mod}}(A,\mathsf{a})
to
\mathsf{a}=0
:

(214)

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}\colon=Z^{\mathrm{mod}}\Big|_{\mathsf{a}=0}\in\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}).

In the notations of the proof of Theorem
5.16
, we have
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}=\mathsf{p}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z}
. Remark
5.17
above implies

(215)

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}=\underline{Z}\big|_{\mathsf{a}=0}\,(1+O(\hbar)).

Proposition 5.19
.

One has

(216)

\displaystyle Z^{\mathrm{glob}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\dim\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\frac{(i\hbar)^{k}}{k!}\left\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial[\delta A]}\right\rangle^{k}\widetilde{Z}^{(k)}\Big|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}=0}

\displaystyle=\left(e^{i\hbar\left\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial[\delta A]}\right\rangle}\widetilde{Z}\right)\Big|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}=[\delta A]=0}.

Here
\dim\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\dim H^{1}_{A}
is the dimension of the connected component of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
containing
[A]
;
\widetilde{Z}^{(k)}
is the
k
-form component of
\widetilde{Z}
, as a form on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

In the notations of the proof of Theorem
5.16
, we have

(217)

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}=\mathsf{p}^{\prime}\widetilde{Z}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathsf{p}(i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\mathsf{K})^{k}\widetilde{Z}.

The chain homotopy
\mathsf{K}
increases the polynomial degree in
\mathsf{a}^{1}
, and in the lowest degree in
\mathsf{a}^{1}
is given by

(218)

\mathsf{K}\omega|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}\rightarrow 0}\sim\frac{1}{\deg\omega}\left\langle\mathsf{a}^{1},\frac{\partial}{\partial[\delta A]}\right\rangle\omega|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}=0},

cf. the homotopy
\delta^{*}
in
[
BCM12
, Section 2]
. On the other hand
\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}
lowers the degree in
\mathsf{a}^{1}
by one and
p
sets
\mathsf{a}^{1}
to zero. So, in the r.h.s. of (
217
), only the constant term in
\mathsf{a}^{1}
contributes, and for the purpose of evaluating the r.h.s.,
\mathsf{K}
can be replaced by its asymptotics (
218
). Formula (
216
) follows.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

Global partition function
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
is related to the perturbative partition function
\underline{Z}
by

(219)

(T\underline{\varphi}^{*}Z^{\mathrm{glob}})(A,\mathsf{a})=\underline{Z}_{A}(\mathsf{a})+i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}}\rho(A,\mathsf{a}).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of (
212
) restricted to form degree zero along
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 5.21

(A path integral formula for
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
)
.

Formula (
216
) can be seen as the perturbative evaluation of the following path integral:

(220)

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}(A)=\int_{H^{2}_{A}[-1]\oplus H^{1}_{A}[1]}\mathcal{D}\mathsf{a}^{2}\,\mathcal{D}\zeta\int_{\mathcal{L}=\Omega_{d_{A}^{*}\mathrm{-ex}}[1]}\mathcal{D}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\\
\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Bigg(S_{CS}(A+i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})+\langle\mathsf{a}^{2},\zeta\rangle\\
+\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{i(\zeta)}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\rangle+\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{i(\zeta)}i(\mathsf{a}^{2})\rangle\Bigg).

The last two terms can also be written as

(221)

\int_{M}\frac{1}{2}\langle i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},H_{\delta A^{\prime}=i(\zeta)}(i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})\rangle,

with
H_{\delta A^{\prime}}
as in (
162b
). Note that in the integral formula (
220
),
\mathsf{a}^{2}
and
\zeta=[\delta A]
become dynamical variables (integrated over).

Figure 12
.

Example of a Feynman graph for
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
(
220
). Dashed edges correspond to
\mathsf{a}^{2}
–
\zeta
propagators; circle vertices correspond to (
221
). Selection rules:
\leq 2
circle vertices on a solid edge,
\leq\dim\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
dashed edges in total. A solid edge not incident to Chern-Simons cubic vertices should have exactly two circle vertices (as in the top part of the picture).

The Feynman graph expansion of
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
has the form

(222)

Z^{\mathrm{glob}}(A)=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}\Big(1+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_Theta.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_goggles.eps}}}\\
+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_Theta_1.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_goggles_1.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_Theta_2.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob_goggles_2.eps}}}+\cdots\Big).

The graphs shown contribute in the order
O(\hbar)
and
\cdots
is of order
\geq 2
in
\hbar
, with graphical conventions as in Figure
12
.

5.5.
Metric dependence of the global partition function

To define
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
we needed to choose a metric
g
on
M
. In this section we analyze the dependence of
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
on this metric. We have shown previously that
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
can be interpreted either as a top form on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
or, equivalently, a half-density on
T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
that does not depend on the fiber coordinates (has degree zero). As such it is trivially closed (w.r.t. de Rham differential or BV Laplacian).

Let

(223)

Z^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}_{g,\phi}(A)\colon=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}Z^{\mathrm{glob}}_{g}(A)

be the renormalized global partition function, with
c(\hbar)
as in (
146
) and
\phi
a framing of
M
.

In this section we are assuming metric-extended smoothness, cf. Definition
5.15
.

The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.22
.

The cohomology class of
Z_{g,\phi}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
is independent of the choice of metric
g
.

Definition 5.23
.

We call the cohomology class
[Z^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}_{g,\phi}]\in H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
the
Chern-Simons volume class
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Consider the following object:

(224)

\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}}\colon=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}\cdot\pi_{*}\Big(\check{Z}\cdot e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\langle[\delta A],\mathsf{a}\rangle}\Big|_{A=A^{\prime},\delta A=\delta A^{\prime}\mathrm{\;harmonic}}\Big)\\
\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathrm{Met}\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H_{A}[1]))

where
\check{Z}
is as in (
170
) and
\pi_{*}
stands for the passage to the quotient by gauge transformations, cf.
201
.

Let

(225)

\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}\colon=\mathsf{p}^{\prime}\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}}\quad\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathrm{Met})\otimes\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime})

with
\mathsf{p}^{\prime}
as in (
211
). Note that
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
is an extension of
Z^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
to a nonhomogeneous form on
\mathrm{Met}
; we denote its
k
-form component by
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}(k)}
.

Proposition 5.24
.

\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
satisfies the following.

(226)

(\delta_{g}-i\hbar\Delta)\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}=0,

(227)

\delta_{g}Z^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}=i\hbar\Delta\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}(1)},

(228)

\displaystyle\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\dim\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\frac{(i\hbar)^{k}}{k!}\left\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial[\delta A]}\right\rangle^{k}\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}(\bullet,k)}\Big|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}=0}

\displaystyle=\left(e^{i\hbar\left\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathsf{a}^{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial[\delta A]}\right\rangle}\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}}\right)\Big|_{\mathsf{a}^{1}=[\delta A]=0}.

Here
\Delta
is the BV Laplacian on half-densities on
T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Superscript
(\bullet,k)
means the component of de Rham degree
k
along
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(and arbitrary degree along
\mathrm{Met}
).

Note that (
227
) immediately implies Theorem
5.22
.

Proof.

First note that, by restricting (
175
) to the diagonal
A=A^{\prime}
and setting
\delta A=\delta A^{\prime}
to be harmonic, one obtains, under the metric-extended smoothness assumption, the equation

(229)

(\delta_{g}+\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}}=0.

Next, consider the contraction (
211
). Note that, by metric-extended smoothness assumption, all maps involved do not depend on the metric.
Tensoring (
211
) with the de Rham complex of
\mathrm{Met}
,
we obtain the contraction

(230)

(\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{p}^{\prime},\mathsf{K}^{\prime})\colon(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathrm{Met}\times\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2},\mathrm{formal}}(H_{A}[1])),\delta_{g}+\nabla^{G}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathsf{a}})\\
\leadsto(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathrm{Met})\otimes\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}),\delta_{g}-i\hbar\Delta).

Since
\mathsf{p}^{\prime}
is a chain map, it sends the cocycle
\overline{Z}^{\mathrm{ren}}
of the complex upstairs to a cocycle
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
of the complex downstairs. This proves (
226
).

Equation (
227
) is the restriction of (
226
) to form degree
1
on
\mathrm{Met}
.

Formula (
228
) for
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
is proven similarly to Proposition
5.19
.
∎

Remark 5.25
.

The path integral formula for
Z^{\mathrm{glob}}
from Remark
5.21
extends – via (
228
) – to the extended global partition function
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}
as follows:

(231)

\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}(A,\mathsf{a}^{2})=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}\cdot\int_{H^{2}_{A}[-1]\oplus H^{1}_{A}[1]}\mathcal{D}\mathsf{a}^{2}\,\mathcal{D}\zeta\int_{\mathcal{L}=\Omega_{d_{A}^{*}\mathrm{-ex}}[1]}\mathcal{D}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\\
\exp\frac{i}{\hbar}\Big(S_{CS}(A+i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})+\langle\mathsf{a}^{2},\zeta\rangle\\
+\int_{M}\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{i(\zeta)}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\rangle+\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},d_{A}G\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{i(\zeta)}i(\mathsf{a}^{2})\rangle}_{-\frac{1}{2}\langle i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},H_{\delta A^{\prime}=i(\zeta)}(i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})\rangle}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},\lambda_{\delta g}\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}}\rangle-\langle\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},\lambda_{\delta g}i(\mathsf{a}^{2})\rangle}_{-\frac{1}{2}\langle i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}},H_{\delta g}(i(\mathsf{a}^{2})+\alpha_{\mathrm{fl}})\rangle}\Big)

with
H_{\delta A^{\prime}}
and
H_{\delta g}
as in (
162b
), (
162c
);
\mathsf{a}^{2}
is interpreted as a vector in
T^{*}_{A}[-1]\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

In particular, the Feynman diagram expansion of the generator
\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}(1)}
in the r.h.s. of (
227
) is

(232)

\check{Z}^{\mathrm{glob,ren}(1)}=\\
e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}c(\hbar)\frac{S_{\mathrm{grav}}(g,\phi)}{2\pi}}\cdot e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{CS}(A)}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\psi_{A,g}}\tau_{A}^{1/2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob1_1.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob1_2.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob1_3.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{Zglob1_4.eps}}}+\cdots\right).

Here the graphical conventions are as in Figure
12
; loose half-edges are decorated by
i(\mathsf{a}^{2})
; black circle vertex is decorated by
H_{\delta g}
. The graphs shown contribute in zeroth order in
\hbar
and
\cdots
is of order
\geq 1
in
\hbar
.

5.5.1.
Relation to the asymptotic expansion conjecture

Now fix
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(N)
and denote
\tau_{k,N}
the
(SU(N)
-Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants
[
RT91
]
.
We recall the statement of the asymptotic expansion conjecture, which we cite from
[
And02
, Conjecture 7.7]

Conjecture 5.26
.

Let
\{c_{0},\ldots,c_{m}\}
be the Chern-Simons invariants of
M
. Then there exist
d_{j}\in\mathbb{Q},\tilde{I}_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},v_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
a_{j}^{e}
for
j=0,\ldots,m
and
e\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
r=k+h^{\vee}
:

(233)

\tau_{k,N}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\sum_{j=0}^{m}e^{\frac{irc_{j}}{2\pi}}r^{d_{j}}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\tilde{I}_{j}}v_{j}\exp\sum_{e=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{e}\left(\frac{r}{2\pi}\right)^{-e}.

Other forms of this conjecture have appeared in the literature, for instance in
[
Res10
, Section 6]
.
We conjecture that if
c_{j}
comes from a union of smooth, irreducible components of the moduli space, then the
j
-th summand above coincides with the integral of Chern-Simons volume class over that preimage. More precisely, fix
\phi
to be the canonical 2-framing of
M
and denote
\mathcal{M}_{j}=S_{CS}^{-1}(c_{j})
. If
\mathcal{M}_{j}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, then we conjecture that
a_{j}^{e}
is given by the coefficient of
\hbar^{e}
(contribution of connected
(e+1)
-loop graphs) in
\log\int_{\mathcal{M}_{j}}Z^{\mathrm{glob,ren}}_{g,\phi}
.
This assumes that at higher loop orders one has to identify
\hbar=\frac{2\pi}{k+h^{\vee}}
(as discussed by Axelrod-Singer
[
AS91
, Section 6]
).

An interesting class of examples where this conjecture could potentially be checked are Seifert fibered homology spheres. For these, the only reducible connection is the trivial one, all other components of the moduli space are closed manifolds (
[
FS90
]
). For this class, the asymptotic expansion conjecture has recently been proven in
[
And+25
]
, where the authors show that the asymptotic expansion is given in terms of integrals over the smooth components of the moduli space. Comparison with this and other results will be addressed in future work.

Appendix A
SDR data and homological perturbation lemma

Here for reader’s convenience we review the definition of SDR (strong deformation retraction) data and the homological perturbation lemma, both well-known in the literature – see e.g.
[
GL89
]
,
[
Cra04
]
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
(V^{\bullet},d_{V})
and
(W^{\bullet},d_{W})
be a pair of cochain complexes.
SDR data
(or an
(i,p,K)
triple
) is a triple of maps

(234)

i\colon W^{\bullet}\rightarrow V^{\bullet},\quad p\colon V^{\bullet}\rightarrow W^{\bullet},\quad K\colon V^{\bullet}\rightarrow V^{\bullet-1}

such that:

•

i
and
p
are chain maps:
d_{V}i=id_{W}
,
d_{W}p=pd_{V}
.

•

i
is an inclusion and
p
a projection, satisfying
pi=\mathrm{id}_{W}
.

•

K
is a chain homotopy between
ip
and
\mathrm{id}_{V}
:
d_{V}K+Kd_{V}=\mathrm{id}-ip
.

•

The following side conditions hold:
K^{2}=Ki=pK=0
.

In particular, existence of SDR data implies that complexes
(V^{\bullet},d_{V})
and
(W^{\bullet},d_{W})
are quasi-isomorphic (with
i
and
p
quasi-isomorphisms); one calls
W^{\bullet}
a
deformation retract
of
V^{\bullet}
.

An important special case is when
(W^{\bullet},d_{W})=(H^{\bullet}(V),0)
is the cohomology of
V
.

A choice of SDR data induces a Hodge-like decomposition

(235)

V=i(W)\oplus\Big(\mathrm{im}(d_{V})\cap\mathrm{ker}(p)\Big)\oplus\mathrm{im}(K).

Here
d_{V}
acts on the first term and maps the third term to the second isomorphically, with
K
the inverse.

Lemma A.2

(Homological perturbation lemma)
.

Let
(V^{\bullet},d_{V})
and
(W^{\bullet},d_{W})
be a pair of complexes with SDR data
(i,p,K)
.
Consider a perturbation of the differential on
V
,
d_{V}\rightarrow\widetilde{d}_{V}=d_{V}+\delta
, for some
\delta\colon V^{\bullet}\rightarrow V^{\bullet+1}
such that
(d_{W}+\delta)^{2}=0
. Then the perturbed complex
(V^{\bullet},d_{V}+\delta)
is quasi-isomorphic to
(W^{\bullet},\widetilde{d}_{W})
with SDR data

(\widetilde{i},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{K})
, where

(236)

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}_{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{W}+p\delta i-p\delta K\delta i+p\delta K\delta K\delta i-\cdots,

(237)

\displaystyle\widetilde{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i-K\delta i+K\delta K\delta i-\cdots,

(238)

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p-p\delta K+p\delta K\delta K-\cdots,

(239)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K-K\delta K+K\delta K\delta K-\cdots,

under the assumption that the geometric progressions above converge.

A.1.
First-order deformations of SDR data

Consider a deformation retraction of a cochain complex
(V^{\bullet},d_{V})
onto its cohomology
(W^{\bullet}=H^{\bullet}(V),0)
and fix SDR data
(i,p,K)
. The Hodge-like decomposition (
235
) in this case is

V=i(W)\oplus V_{d\mathrm{-exact}}\oplus V_{K\mathrm{-exact}}
.

Lemma A.3
.
54
54

54

See
[
Mne08
]
,
[
CM08
]
,
[
CMR20
]
.

A general infinitesimal deformation of
(i,p,K)
, in the class of SDR data where
p|_{V_{d\mathrm{-closed}}}
is the standard projection of closed elements to cohomology classes, has the form

(240)

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle i-\varepsilon d_{V}\mathsf{I},

(241)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle p-\varepsilon\mathsf{P}d_{V},

(242)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle K+\varepsilon([d_{V},\Lambda]+i\mathsf{P}+\mathsf{I}p),

with
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{P},\Lambda
arbitrary maps

(243)

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}\colon W^{\bullet}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle V^{\bullet-1}_{K\mathrm{-exact}},

(244)

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}\colon V^{\bullet}_{d\mathrm{-exact}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle W^{\bullet-1},

(245)

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon V^{\bullet}_{d\mathrm{-exact}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle V^{\bullet-2}_{K\mathrm{-exact}}

and
\varepsilon
the deformation parameter.

For the applications of this paper, we parametrize the maps
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{P}
above as

(246)

\mathsf{I}=\mathbb{I}i,\quad\mathsf{P}=p\mathbb{P},

with

(247)

\mathbb{I}\colon V^{\bullet}\rightarrow V^{\bullet-1}_{K\mathrm{-exact}},\quad\mathbb{P}\colon V^{\bullet}_{d\mathrm{-exact}}\rightarrow V^{\bullet-1}

arbitrary maps.

Appendix B
Variation of desynchronized Hodge SDR data

In this section we consider the variation of the SDR data
(i_{A,A^{\prime}},p_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}})
given by the Hodge decomposition associated to a pair of close flat connections
(A,A^{\prime})
and the metric
g
, in the direction of the three parameters
(A,A^{\prime},g)
.

Recall that the the metric induces on the complex of
\mathfrak{g}
-valued differential forms the pairing

(248)

\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})}=\int_{M}\langle\alpha,*\beta\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}

and associated with it the operator
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
, the formal adjoint of
d_{A^{\prime}}
, the twisted, desynchronized Hodge-de Rham Laplacian
\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}:=(d_{A}+d^{*}_{A^{\prime}})^{2}
, the projection
P_{A,A^{\prime}}
to
\ker\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}
along
\operatorname{im}\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}
, and the Green’s operator of the Hodge-de Rham Laplacian,
G_{A,A^{\prime}}=(\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}})^{-1}
, satisfying

\Delta_{A,A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=G_{A,A^{\prime}}\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}=\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Recall that the SDR data
(i_{A,A^{\prime}},p_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}})
specified by the Hodge decomposition of the twisted de Rham complex is given by

(249)

\displaystyle i_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon H^{\bullet}_{A}\to\Omega^{\bullet},

\displaystyle i_{A,A^{\prime}}[\alpha]=P_{A,A^{\prime}}\alpha

(250)

\displaystyle p_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}\to H^{\bullet}_{A},

\displaystyle p_{A,A^{\prime}}\beta=\left[P_{A,A^{\prime}}\beta\right]

(251)

\displaystyle K_{A,A^{\prime}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}\to\Omega^{\bullet-1},

\displaystyle K_{A,A^{\prime}}=d^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}

for
\alpha
a
d_{A}
-closed form and
\beta
any
\mathfrak{g}
-valued form.

B.1.
Changing the kinetic operator

Lemma B.1

(Changing the kinetic operator)
.

Let
A_{t}\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
a path of smooth flat connections such that
(A_{t},A^{\prime})
is close for all
t
and
A_{0}=A
. Denote
\dot{A_{0}}=\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.
Then we have

(252)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\right\},

(253)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}},

(254)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}{K}_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Since
d_{A_{t}}=d+\mathrm{ad}_{A_{t}}
, we have
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}{d}_{A_{t}}=\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
. Equation (
252
) then follows directly from rewriting the Laplacian as
\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}=\left\{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},d_{A}\right\}
.

To prove equation
(
253
)
, we differentiate the equations
\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=P^{2}_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}-P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=0
. Differentiating the first one we obtain

\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}+\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}=0,

which yields, after composing with
G_{A,A^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\left(\left\{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\right\}\right)P_{A,A^{\prime}}

(using (
252
))

\displaystyle=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}d^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{(since }d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}=0)

(255)

\displaystyle=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{(since }d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\text{ commutes with }G_{A,A^{\prime}}).

Similarly, differentiating
P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=0
we obtain

(256)

\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})=-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Differentiating
P^{2}_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}-P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=0
we obtain

P_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}+\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=0

or

(257)

P_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}).

Finally, we can compute, using (
255
),(
256
), (
257
)

\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=-\left(K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\right)P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\right)

which proves equation (
253
).

Finally, let us prove (
254
). Remember that we have
K_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
and hence

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}{K}_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}{G_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}}\right).

On the other hand, using that
G_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=(\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}+P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}})^{-1}
, we have

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}G_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=-G_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{t=0}}}(\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}+P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}})G_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}.

Using (
252
) and (
253
) we obtain

(258)

\dot{G}_{A,A^{\prime}}=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\big(\left\{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\right\}-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\big)G_{A,A^{\prime}}.

After applying
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
, only the first term survives and yields

\dot{K}_{A,A^{\prime}}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\dot{G}_{A,A^{\prime}}-d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\left\{d^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\right\}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}

since
\left(d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\right)^{2}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}=0
and
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
and
G_{A,A^{\prime}}
commute.
∎

B.2.
Changing the gauge-fixing operator

Proposition B.2

(Changing the gauge-fixing operator)
.

Let
A_{t}^{\prime}\colon(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\to\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
a path of smooth flat connections with
A^{\prime}_{0}=A^{\prime}
such that
(A,A^{\prime}_{t})
is flat for all
t
. Denote
\dot{A_{0}}=\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(M,\mathfrak{g})
. We denote by
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}^{*}
the formal adjoint of
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
and
K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}=d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Then we have

(259)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}=\left\{d_{A},\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\right\},

(260)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}P_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}=-K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}},

(261)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}{K}_{A,A^{\prime}_{t}}=\left[d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}^{*}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\right]+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Again, (
259
) follows directly from writing the Laplacian as
\Delta_{A_{t}}=\left\{d_{A},d^{*}_{A^{\prime}_{t}}\right\}
. In exactly the same way as above, we then obtain

\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}+\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})=-K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}},

proving (
260
). Using (
259
),(
260
), we obtain

(262)

\dot{G}_{A,A^{\prime}}=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\big(\left\{d_{A^{\prime}},\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\right\}-K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\big)G_{A,A^{\prime}}.

After applying
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
, the third term vanishes. The first one is

d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\left\{d_{A},\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\right\}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A}\\
=(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}-d_{A,A^{\prime}}d^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}})\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A,A^{\prime}}\\
=\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}-\left[d_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\right].

The second one is

d_{A^{\prime}}^{*}G_{A,A^{\prime}}K^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}-d_{A}d_{A^{\prime}}^{*})G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\\
=G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-d_{A}d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

where we have used that
P_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=P_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Using Lemma
B.3
below, the compositions
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}=P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}0
.

The variation of
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
is finally given by

\dot{K}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\dot{G}_{A,A^{\prime}}\\
=P_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+\left[d_{A,A^{\prime}},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\right]+G_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}P_{A,A^{\prime}}

proving (
261
).
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
A_{0}
be a flat connection and
\alpha

d_{A_{0}}
-closed 1-form. Then

•

The map
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
maps
d_{A_{0}}
-closed forms to
d_{A_{0}}
-closed forms.

•

If
[A_{0}]
defines a smooth point in the moduli space,
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
maps all
d_{A_{0}}
-closed forms to
d_{A_{0}}
-
exact
forms.

•

Dually,
\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}
maps
d^{*}_{A_{0}}
-closed form to
d^{*}_{A_{0}}
-closed forms, and
d^{*}_{A_{0}}
-exact forms if
[A_{0}]
is a smooth point.

Proof.

The first point is obvious since the bracket is compatible with the differential. For the second point, notice that
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
always maps exact forms to exact forms. The smoothness assumption implies that for any close
A^{\prime}
,
l_{2}([\alpha],\bullet)=p_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}\alpha
vanishes on harmonic forms, hence
\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}
maps harmonic forms into exact forms. To prove the last point, let
\beta
be a coclosed form and
\gamma
an exact form. Then

\langle\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\beta,\gamma\rangle=\langle\beta,\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}\gamma\rangle=0

since coclosed forms are orthogonal to exact forms. Hence
\mathrm{ad}^{*}_{\alpha}\beta
is also coclosed. If
[A_{0}]
is smooth we can let
\gamma
be any closed form, hence
\langle\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}^{*}\beta\rangle
is coexact in this case.
∎

We are also interested in the variations of
i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
and
p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
. However, remember that
i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}\colon H_{A_{t}}(M,\mathfrak{g})\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, so all the
i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
are defined a priori on different spaces. Notice that we have the maps

(263)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{\bullet}_{A_{0}}(M,\mathfrak{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{\bullet}_{A_{t}}(M,\mathfrak{g})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}i_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A_{0},A^{\prime}}i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}}

Using these maps to compare the different
i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
and
p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
, we have the following result:

Lemma B.4
.

With notation as in Lemma
B.1
, we have

(264)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}i_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}i_{A,A^{\prime}},

(265)

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\frac{d}{dt}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{t=0}}p_{A,A^{\prime}}i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-p_{A,A^{\prime}}(\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha}K_{A,A^{\prime}}).

Proof.

Notice that
i_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}p_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}=P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}}
. Then the formulae follow immediately from equation (
253
).
∎

In general, the maps (
263
) are neither injective nor surjective. However, at smooth points, the following is true.

Proposition B.5
.

Suppose
[A_{0}]
is smooth. Then for small
t
, the maps (
263
) are isomorphisms.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that for small
t
the restriction of
P_{A_{t}}
to
A_{0}
-harmonic forms is an isomorphism. This follows from Proposition
2.15
.
∎

We remark that the maps (
263
) coincide with the cohomology comparison maps
\mathfrak{B}_{A_{t}\leftarrow A_{0},A^{\prime}}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{A_{0}\leftarrow A_{t},A^{\prime}}
, cf. Section
2.5.4
. This follows from comparing (
264
) with the connection
\nabla^{\mathrm{Harm}}
(
82
).

Remark B.6
.

The formulae (
253
), (
260
) for
\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}P_{A_{t},A^{\prime}_{t}}
can also be obtained as follows:
One has

(266)

P=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}e^{-T\Delta_{A_{t},A^{\prime}_{t}}},

hence

(267)

\dot{P}=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{T}dt\,e^{-t\Delta}(-\dot{\Delta})e^{-(T-t)\Delta}.

The
T\rightarrow\infty
asymptotics of the integral in the r.h.s. comes from two regions (a)
t\ll T
, (b)
T-t\ll T
– neighborhoods of the endpoints of the integration interval
[0,T]
(the bulk of the interval does not contribute since
P\dot{\Delta}P=0
):

(268)

\dot{P}=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{-t\Delta}\right)(-\dot{\Delta})e^{-\infty\cdot\Delta}+e^{-\infty\cdot\Delta}(-\dot{\Delta})\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{-t\Delta}\right)\\
=-G\dot{\Delta}P-P\dot{\Delta}G.

Here we are suppressing the subscripts
A,A^{\prime}
for
P
,
\Delta
,
G
;
e^{-\infty\cdot\Delta}
is a shorthand for the r.h.s. of (
266
).

Figure 13
.
Terms in the formula (
268
) for
\delta P
correspond to splitting the interval by a point (a) close to the left endpoint, (c) far from both endpoints (the respective contribution is zero), (b) close to the right endpoint.

B.3.
Metric dependence

Let
g_{t},t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
be a smooth 1-parameter family of Riemannian metrics and denote
\dot{g}=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}g_{t}\in\Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(T^{*}M))
. By a partial contraction with
g^{-1}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(TM)
we obtain a endomorphism of the tangent bundle, i.e. a vector-field valued 1-form

(269)

\mu=g^{-1}\dot{g}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(TM))\cong\Gamma(TM\otimes T^{*}M)=\Omega^{1}(M,TM).

Lemma B.7
.

Denote
\lambda=\star^{-1}\dot{\star}\colon\Omega^{p}(M)\to\Omega^{p}(M)
, then

(270)

\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\mu-\iota_{\mu}.

Proof.

Straightforward computation in local coordinates.
∎

It is well known (e.g.
[
RS71
]
) that we have

(271)

\dot{d}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}=[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda].

Analogously to Proposition
B.2
, we then have the following statements:

Proposition B.8
.

Let
g_{t}
be a smooth family of Riemannian metrics on
M
, and
\lambda=\star^{-1}\dot{\star}
as above, extended to act on Lie-algebra valued differential forms by tensoring with the identity on
\mathfrak{g}
. Also, let
(A,A^{\prime})
be a pair of close flat connections on
M
. Then, we have

(272)

\displaystyle\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]d_{A}+d_{A}[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda],

(273)

\displaystyle\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-[d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}],

(274)

\displaystyle\dot{K}_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-[d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}]-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}+K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Equation (
272
) follows immediately from (
271
)
To prove (
273
), we proceed as above in noticing that

(275)

\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=(\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}})\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}.

By using (
272
), we obtain

(276)

\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}=-G_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A}d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=-[d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}]

where have also used that
d_{A}
and
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}
commute with
G_{A,A^{\prime}}
and annihilate
P_{A,A^{\prime}}
.
Finally, for the variation of
K_{A,A^{\prime}}
(
274
) we obtain

(277)

\dot{K}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\underbrace{\dot{d}^{*}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}}_{=:I}-\underbrace{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{\Delta}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}}_{=:II}-\underbrace{d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}\dot{P}_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}}_{=:III}=I-II-III.

Let us look at the three terms separately. From (
271
), we get

(278)

I=[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]G_{A_{0}}.

For the second term, we obtain

(279)

II=d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}([d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]d_{A}+d_{A}[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda])G_{A,A^{\prime}}\\
=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A}+d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]G_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Notice that
\Delta_{A,A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}
implies
d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}=-d_{A}d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}G_{A,A^{\prime}}+\mathrm{id}-P_{A,A^{\prime}}
and therefore

d^{*}_{A^{\prime}}d_{A}G_{A,A^{\prime}}[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]G_{A,A^{\prime}}=d_{A}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}+[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]G_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}},

so that

(280)

II=[d_{A},K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}]+[d^{*}_{A^{\prime}},\lambda]G_{A,A^{\prime}}+P_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda K_{A,A^{\prime}}.

Finally, by using (
276
) we can rewrite
III
as

(281)

III=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}d_{A}K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}=-K_{A,A^{\prime}}\lambda P_{A,A^{\prime}}

since, suppressing indices,
KdK=K(Kd+\mathrm{id}-P)=K
by
K^{2}=KP=0
. Now (
274
) follows from (
277
) by using (
278
),(
279
),(
281
).
∎

Appendix C
Construction of extended
(i,p,K)
triples from families

One can obtain formulae (
137
) and Lemma
4.14
from homological perturbation theory, as follows. Suppose
Q_{q}
is a good gauge fixing operator for
d_{A}
for
q\in\mathbb{GF}
, a smooth (but possibly infinite-dimensional) manifold. For fixed
q\in\mathbb{GF}
, one has the SDR data
(i_{q},p_{q},K_{q})
from (
76
). These assemble into SDR data

(\overline{i},\overline{p},\overline{K})
for
d_{A}
, considered as a differential on
\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g})
:

(282)

\displaystyle\overline{K}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g};d_{A})

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g};d_{A}),

\displaystyle\overline{i}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{GF},H_{A}(M,\mathfrak{g}))

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g};d_{A}),

\displaystyle\overline{p}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g};d_{A})

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{GF},H_{A}(M,\mathfrak{g})).

Similarly,
Q_{q}
and the Green’s function
G_{q}
assemble into operators
\overline{Q},\overline{G}
on
\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g})
.

We can now deform the differential
d_{A}
to the (twisted) de Rham differential on
M\times\mathbb{GF}
, by the de Rham differential
\delta_{q}
in the direction of
\mathbb{GF}
. Note that since
\delta_{q}
increases the de Rham degree in
\mathbb{GF}
by 1, the map

1+\delta_{q}\overline{K}
is invertible, we denote

(283)

X:=(1+\delta_{q}\overline{K})^{-1}\delta_{q}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-\delta_{q}\overline{K})^{k}\delta_{q}=\delta_{q}-\delta_{q}\overline{K}\delta_{q}+\cdots

(this sum is finite since
\overline{K}
decreases the form degree along
M
by 1).
We then obtain perturbed SDR data (see Appendix
A
)

(284)

\displaystyle\widetilde{i}

\displaystyle=\overline{i}-\overline{K}X\overline{i}=\overline{i}-\widetilde{K}\delta_{q}\overline{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}

\displaystyle=\overline{p}-\overline{p}X\overline{K}=\overline{p}-\overline{p}\delta_{q}\widetilde{K},

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}

\displaystyle=\overline{K}-\overline{K}X\overline{K},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta_{q}}

\displaystyle=\overline{p}X\overline{i}=\overline{p}\delta_{q}\overline{i}-\overline{p}\delta_{q}\widetilde{K}\delta_{q}\overline{i}.

By Lemma
A.2
,
(\widetilde{i},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{K})
form SDR data between the complexes
\Omega^{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{GF},\mathfrak{g};d_{A}+\delta_{q})
and
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbb{GF},H_{A}(M,\mathfrak{g});\widetilde{\delta}_{q}).

Proposition C.1
.

One can rewrite formulae (
284
) as follows:

(285)

\displaystyle\widetilde{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-\overline{G}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q}))^{k}\overline{i},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{p}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{K}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta_{q}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{q}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})d_{A}\overline{G}(-\overline{G}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q}))^{k}\overline{i}.

Note that, for degree reasons, only
k=0,1,2
terms survive in
\widetilde{i},\widetilde{p},\widetilde{K}
and only
k=1
term survives in
\widetilde{\delta}_{q}
.

In particular, for

(286)

\mathbb{GF}=\{A^{\prime}\in\mathrm{FC}^{\prime}|(A,A^{\prime})\text{ close}\},

we have
\widetilde{i}=\widehat{i},\widetilde{p}=\widehat{p},\widetilde{K}=\widehat{K},\widetilde{\delta_{q}}=\delta_{A^{\prime}}+\widehat{\Theta}
, with
(\widehat{i},\widehat{p},\widehat{K},\widehat{\Theta})
given by (
137
). Lemma
4.14
then follows from the fact that the deformed
(i,p,K)
triple is again an
(i,p,K)
triple.

Proof.

One can simplify formulae (
284
) by noticing that

(287)

[\delta_{q},\overline{K}]=\delta_{q}\overline{K}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{GF},\operatorname{End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})))

where the right hand side acts as a multiplication operator on differential forms in the
\mathbb{GF}
direction.
We are using the notations where for an operator
x\in\{\overline{K},\overline{i},\overline{p},\overline{Q}\}
,
(\delta_{q}x)
stands for
[\delta_{q},x]
. By induction, one proves that

(288)

\widetilde{K}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-\overline{K}\delta_{q})^{k}\overline{K}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{K}(-\delta_{q}\overline{K})^{k}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-\delta_{q}\overline{K})^{k}\overline{K}

where in the second equality we have used that
\delta_{q}\overline{K}
and
\overline{K}
commute as a consequence of
\overline{K}^{2}=0
.
Using further that
(\overline{K})_{q}=Q_{q}G_{q}
, we have
(\delta_{q}\overline{K})_{q}=(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})_{q}G_{q}-Q_{q}(\delta_{q}\overline{G})_{q}
, but using that
K_{q}Q_{q}=0
we then obtain

(289)

\widetilde{K}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{K}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{G}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}\overline{Q}.

This proves the third equation in (
285
)
We can then also rewrite the first two equations in (
284
) by realizing that

(290)

\overline{Q}\delta_{q}\overline{i}=\overline{Q}(\delta_{q}\overline{i})=(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{i}

and

(291)

\overline{p}\delta_{q}\overline{Q}=-(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\overline{Q}=\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q}),

combining (
284
), (
289
),(
290
) we get

\widetilde{i}=\overline{i}-\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{G}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}\overline{Q}\delta_{q}\overline{i}\\
=\overline{i}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\overline{G}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}(-\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{i}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(-\overline{G}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q}))^{k}\overline{i}

which proves the first equation in (
285
). Combining (
284
), (
289
) and (
291
), we obtain

\widetilde{p}=\overline{p}-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G}\sum_{k\geq 0}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}\\
=\overline{p}-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G}\sum_{k\geq 0}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k}=\overline{p}\sum_{k\geq 0}(-(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})\overline{G})^{k},

which proves the second equation in (
285
). Finally, we focus on the last equation. The first term is simply

\overline{p}\delta_{q}\overline{i}=\underbrace{\overline{p}\overline{i}}_{=1_{H^{\bullet}(M,\mathfrak{g})}}\delta_{q}+\underbrace{\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{i})}_{=0}=\delta_{q}.

For the second term, notice that we have
\overline{p}\widetilde{K}=0
and therefore

\overline{p}\delta_{q}\widetilde{K}\delta_{q}\overline{i}=-(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\widetilde{K}\delta_{q}\overline{i}=(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\sum_{k\geq 1}(-\overline{G}(\delta_{q}\widehat{Q}_{0}))^{k}\overline{i}.

The proof of the last equation in (
285
) now follows from

\delta_{q}\overline{p}=-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})d_{A}\overline{G},

which in turn can be proved by deriving the identity
\overline{p}\overline{Q}=0
:

(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\overline{Q}=-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})

and then composing both sides with
d_{A}\overline{G}
on the left:

-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{Q})d_{A}\overline{G}=(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\overline{Q}d_{A}\overline{G}=-(\delta_{q}\overline{p})(d_{A}\overline{Q}\,\overline{G}-\mathrm{id}+\overline{i}\overline{p})=\delta_{q}\overline{p},

because
(\delta_{q}\overline{p})d_{A}=\delta_{q}(\overline{p}d_{A})=0
and
(\delta_{q}\overline{p})\overline{i}=-\overline{p}(\delta_{q}\overline{i})=0
, because changing
q
shifts representatives of cohomology by
d_{A}
-exact terms and
\overline{p}d_{A}=0
.
∎

Appendix D
Some technical proofs

D.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.9

Proof.

For point i), notice that because the assumption of boundedness of
K_{A_{0}}
in a Banach norm, by the Banach inverse function theorem the inverse exists in a neighborhood of every point
\delta
where the differential of
\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}
,

(292)

(d\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}})_{\delta}=\mathrm{Id}+K_{A_{0}}\mathrm{ad}_{\delta}\colon\Omega^{1}\to\Omega^{1}

is invertible. In particular, by the triangle inequality this happens when the operator norm of
K_{A_{0}}\delta
is less than one.

For point ii), we have to show
\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}(\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha))=\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}(\alpha)=\alpha.
To see that
\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}(\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha))=\alpha,
recall that the coefficients
\alpha^{(j)}
of
\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}
are given by summing over binary trees with
j
leaves, with prefactor
1/2^{j+1}
and sign
(-1)^{j+1}
. When evaluating
\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}(\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha)
we are placing
\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha)
instead on every leaf. But since
\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha)=\alpha+\frac{1}{2}K_{A_{0}}[\alpha,\alpha]
, we can express
\widetilde{\delta}_{A_{0}}(\widetilde{\kappa}_{A_{0}}(\alpha))
again as a sum over binary trees
T^{\prime}
evaluated according to the same rules, but with a different combinatorial factor
c_{T^{\prime}}
, allowing for the fact that the same tree
T^{\prime}
could arise from several different trees
T
. See Figure
14
for an example.

T_{1}
K_{A_{0}}
